# Daily Papers
The project automatically fetches the latest papers from arXiv based on keywords.

The subheadings in the README file represent the search keywords.

Only the most recent articles for each keyword are retained, up to a maximum of 100 papers.

You can click the 'Watch' button to receive daily email notifications.

Last update: 2026-10-22

## Multimodal Learning

| **[Reliability-Aware Probabilistic Fusion for Multimodal Learning with Incomplete Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29621v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal inputs may be missing or degraded, raising distinct questions of evidence availability and trust. We introduce a reliability-aware Gaussian Product-of-Experts (PoE) in which an input-dependent reliability weight tempers each modality's latent precision, while missing experts are omitted. We characterize how reliability and availability affect posterior information and expert influence. Across two clinical multimodal tasks, the full framework remains competitive with complete inputs and achieves its clearest mean-AUROC advantage under modality absence, particularly on HAIM-MIMIC-MM. Ablations show a substantial gain from missing-aware training and an additional HAIM improvement from reliability weighting under matched PoE, missing-aware, and consistency training. Unseen test-time corruptions are used diagnostically and reveal modality-dependent reliability-weight and latent-uncertainty responses, including failure under severe degradation. Thus, reliability weighting provides an explicit evidence-control mechanism, while learning dependable quality signals remains an open challenge.</p></details> |  |
| **[On the Complementarity of Quantum and Classical Features: Adaptive Hybrid Quantum-Classical Feature Fusion for Breast Cancer Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.22903v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The integration of quantum machine learning with classical deep learning offers promising avenues for medical image analysis by mapping data into high-dimensional Hilbert spaces. However, unifying these paradigms remains challenging due to optimization asymmetries. We propose a hybrid quantum-classical architecture for breast cancer diagnosis based on a dual-branch feature-extraction pipeline. Our framework unifies complementary representations from classical models and trainable or deterministic (non-trainable) quantum circuits. Three fusion strategies are introduced: Static Hybrid Fusion (SHF) for offline extraction, Dynamic Hybrid Fusion (DHF) for end-to-end co-adaptation, and Temperature-Scaled Hybrid Fusion (TSHF). Inspired by multimodal learning, TSHF uses a learnable scalar to balance branch gradient dynamics and address optimization bottlenecks. We evaluate BreastMNIST, INbreast, and BUS-UCLM, spanning ultrasound and mammography, using ResNet-18 and a shallow CNN, with five independent runs per configuration. Fusion improves over the strongest classical baseline on all three benchmarks. On BreastMNIST, TSHF with ResNet-18 and a non-trainable circuit achieves 88.33% accuracy, 92.09% F1-score, and 92.09% AUC-ROC, versus 84.74% accuracy and 87.08% AUC-ROC for ResNet-18 alone. On INbreast, SHF with the same pairing performs best, at 70.42% accuracy, 72.96% F1-score, and 80.28% AUC-ROC. On BUS-UCLM, SHF with a trainable circuit achieves 78.46% accuracy and 84.66% F1-score, over seven accuracy points above the best standalone branch. Adaptive fusion is not universally beneficial: static fusion performs best on the two smaller datasets, suggesting an additional estimation burden from co-adaptation and learned calibration under limited data. Clinical applicability requires validation beyond the small, low-resolution datasets and simulated quantum circuits considered here.</p></details> | <details><summary>41 pa...</summary><p>41 pages, 16 figures. This manuscript is a preprint under review at Artificial Intelligence in Medicine</p></details> |
| **[Beyond Invariance: Factor-wise Selective Treatment of Shifts for Multimodal Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21403v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Distribution shifts pose a fundamental challenge to robust multimodal learning. Existing methods typically promote invariance by suppressing environment-dependent variations, implicitly applying a uniform treatment to shifted information. However, shifted information can have different predictive consequences: some contributes little to prediction, some remains reliably informative, and some exhibits environment-dependent predictive effects. This raises a fundamental question: which shifted information should be suppressed, preserved, or attenuated? To address this question, we propose FaShift, a factor-wise shift framework that diagnoses latent representation factors according to their distributional changes, intervention effects, and cross-environment stability. By factorizing multimodal representations and evaluating these complementary properties, FaShift distinguishes non-predictive factors, stably predictive factors, and predictively useful but unstable factors. It then selectively suppresses, preserves, or attenuates their contributions, rather than enforcing uniform invariance. Experiments on MOSI, MOSEI, and CH-SIMS show that FaShift achieves the best ID accuracy and F1 across all three datasets. Compared with the strongest competitors reporting OOD results, it improves OOD accuracy/F1 by 1.5/3.6 points on MOSI and 1.7/2.7 points on MOSEI, while achieving the best reported OOD accuracy and F1 on CH-SIMS.</p></details> |  |
| **[Reusing Multimodal Agents: Efficient Evaluation of Sound Designs in Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27670v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This paper introduces a novel framework for efficiently evaluating in-game sound designs using multimodal learning and reinforcement learning. Sound design significantly influences gameplay and accessibility; however, objective evaluation methods, defined here as automated assessments based on agent performance rather than human ratings, often require retraining agents from scratch for each sound design. Our approach first pre-trains a multimodal encoder that encodes visual and auditory information into a shared latent space. A reinforcement learning agent is then trained using this latent representation. We fine-tune the multimodal encoder for each sound design while keeping the trained policy fixed, allowing us to isolate the effects of sound changes on agent behavior. To evaluate the proposed method, we conducted experiments on games from multiple genres, comparing the performance of blind agents (agents using only sound input) under various sound designs. Experimental results suggest that this approach can detect differences in agent performance caused by degraded or altered sound design. Moreover, we significantly reduce the computational cost compared to retraining the agent from scratch. Experimental results suggest that this approach provides a practical and scalable solution for evaluating in-game sound designs. The source code, data and supplementary document are available: https://github.com/thainv0212/tog-reusing-agents.</p></details> |  |
| **[VLX-Go: Robust Embodied Tracking through Multi-Stage Learning and On-Policy Refinement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19361v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Embodied visual tracking requires a robot to identify a designated target from visual observations and control its own motion to follow that target through an environment. Following an isolated person is a comparatively simple demonstration of embodied visual tracking, but following the intended person among distractors remains challenging. Existing methods exhibit a substantial drop in success from single-target to distractor tracking, motivating a focus on the shared spaces that person-following robots must navigate. We present VLX-GO, a vision-language waypoint policy built around the hypothesis that visual and language representations should be aligned before tracking-specific motion is learned. The policy combines complementary frozen visual encoders, multiscale temporal context, a transformer backbone, and a lightweight head for robot-centric waypoint prediction. Its training progresses through vision--language alignment, multimodal reasoning mid-training, and tracking specialization on 19,532 oracle episodes. Compared with 41.54% distractor-tracking success for our earlier OmTrackVLA model, the complete multi-stage recipe achieves 55.80%. We then explore on-policy refinement using filtered oracle labels for states visited by the policy. This preliminary study adds 2.42 percentage points of distractor-tracking success, reaching 58.22%, while reducing collision rate by 1.99 pts. The final policy records the highest distractor- and ambiguous-tracking success rates among the compared methods. These results support staged multimodal learning as a promising direction for tracking among distractors and motivate further study of on-policy refinement for person following in shared spaces. Code is available at https://github.com/om-ai-lab/VLX-Go.</p></details> | tech report |
| **[CheXFuse: Guided Differential Joint Attention Fusion for Robust Multimodal Chest X-Ray Classification in Data-Scarce Medical Settings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25617v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A central challenge in multimodal learning is how to fuse modalities that differ substantially in predictive strength. When one modality provides a much stronger representation than another, indiscriminate cross-modal interaction can introduce weak, redundant, or unstable signals, limiting or even degrading performance relative to the strongest unimodal representation. To address this problem, we propose CheXFuse, a multimodal fusion framework based on Guided Differential Joint Attention (GuDJA). CheXFuse combines image and text token representations within a joint multimodal sequence, where learned token guidance emphasizes informative representations and differential attention reduces less useful attention responses. We evaluate CheXFuse on the Indiana University (IU) Chest X-ray dataset, using binary (normal vs. abnormal) classification as the downstream task. Experiments are conducted under both original-report and masked-report settings, with the latter serving as a controlled stress test under reduced textual informativeness. CheXFuse with GuDJA achieves the strongest mean overall performance across the evaluated baselines and ablations, reaching 95.73% accuracy, 95.79% F1-score, 96.54% recall, and 95.28% sensitivity at 95% specificity (Sens@95Spec) with original reports, and 95.58%, 95.60%, 95.60%, and 94.34%, respectively, under masked reports. These results suggest that GuDJA provides a promising fusion strategy under asymmetric or partially reduced modality informativeness.</p></details> | IEEE ICDM DMBIH 2026 |
| **[MM-LeJEPA: Correlated Gaussian Regularization for Multimodal Joint-Embedding Predictive Architectures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24681v1)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LeJEPA derives an isotropic Gaussian embedding target by minimizing, under its task assumptions, a Fisher information criterion that controls the task-averaged squared prediction bias. This enables unimodal self-supervised learning without reconstruction decoders, exponential-moving-average teachers, or contrastive negatives. However, its isotropic task prior does not account for the cross-modal structure of multimodal tasks that read shared content. We introduce MM-LeJEPA by extending LeJEPA's task assumptions to multimodal learning. Specifically, we model multimodal tasks as strength-weighted combinations of components that read independent information from the two modalities and components that read the same content from both. Under explicit gradient-moment, correspondence, and symmetry assumptions, we derive a correlated Gaussian target that minimizes a weighted Fisher information functional under a fixed total variance constraint. An analytic transform adapts Sketched Isotropic Gaussian Regularization (SIGReg) to this target, yielding a two-term objective that combines distributional regularization with local-to-global prediction. The extension adds a single scalar hyperparameter, the target cross-modal correlation, and retains the simplicity of LeJEPA. On public audio-visual classification benchmarks, our results are competitive with representative open-source methods. Code will be released.</p></details> |  |
| **[AdamPM: Memory-Efficient LLM Optimization via Moment Pooling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24598v1)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Adam is widely used for training large language models (LLMs) owing to its strong optimization performance and training stability, but its element-wise estimation for the first and second moments incur substantial memory overhead. Although numerous memory-efficient optimizers have been proposed, many incur additional computational overhead or require additional tuning of hyperparameters. Consequently, Adam remains a dominant and reliable choice for LLM pre-training. We propose AdamPM (Adam with Pooled Moments), a simple and memory-efficient variant of Adam that groups adjacent moment entries along the row or column dimension and represents each group by a single shared mean. AdamPM can well inherit Adam's hyperparameter settings and is readily integrated into existing Adam optimizer implementations, with less memory overhead. We systematically investigate pooling of both first and second moment estimates and identify second moment pooling as the effective design. With a group size of $s$, AdamPM reduces second moment memory to $1/s$ of the original requirement. Extensive experiments on language model pretraining, fine-tuning and multimodal learning demonstrate that AdamPM maintains model performance and training speed while substantially reducing optimizer-state memory. Our code is available at \url{https://github.com/jixiaowei95/AdamPM}.</p></details> | 19 pages |
| **[MSCR: Jointly Balancing Utilization Across Modalities and Discovering Synergistic Information](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24395v1)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In multimodal learning, modality imbalance often lets one modality dominate optimization and keeps the model from fully exploiting cross-modal interactions. Balance-oriented methods such as the Multimodal Competition Regularizer (MCR) perform well by alleviating this imbalance during training. These dynamic balancing methods, however, mainly adjust modality contributions and do not consider how imbalance affects the formation of synergistic information during fusion. Synergy is a major source of multimodal advantage, because it is predictive gain that cannot be recovered from unimodal evidence alone. We propose the Multimodal Synergistic Competition Regularizer (MSCR), a fusion method that jointly promotes balanced modality utilization and the discovery of synergistic information. MSCR has two complementary objectives. An anti-dominance objective suppresses excessive unimodal influence and leaves room for synergistic information, and a synergy objective encourages the fused predictor to outperform a non-synergistic unimodal reference. An adaptive gate further concentrates the regularization on samples that need stronger collaborative modeling. Experiments on five benchmarks show that MSCR consistently improves performance over MCR and strengthens synergistic capability. It raises conditional recovery, the accuracy of the fused prediction on samples that the unimodal predictors misclassify, as well as the share of the model's output that InterSHAP attributes to cross-modal interactions. Its gain in conditional recovery persists when the fusion module is Transformer-based. Code is available at https://github.com/chenxinyu991-collab/MSCR.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |
| **[Cattle-CLIP: A Multimodal Framework for Dairy Cattle Behaviour Recognition from Video](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.09203v3)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Monitoring cattle behaviour provides valuable insights into animal health, productivity and welfare. However, robust behaviour recognition in real-world farm environments remains challenging because well-annotated livestock video datasets are scarce and agricultural surveillance footage differs substantially from large-scale pretraining data. To address these challenges, we propose Cattle-CLIP, a domain-adaptive vision-language framework based on Contrastive Language-Image Pretraining (CLIP), which reformulates cattle behaviour recognition as a cross-modal semantic alignment task. Cattle-CLIP incorporates a temporal integration module that extends image-level contrastive pretraining to video-based behaviour understanding and promotes consistent semantic alignment across time. To reduce domain discrepancy, customised data augmentation and behaviour-oriented prompts are employed. We also construct CattleBehaviours6, a curated dataset containing 1,905 annotated clips from six indoor behaviours: feeding, drinking, standing-self-grooming, standing-ruminating, lying-self-grooming and lying-ruminating. The dataset provides standardised ethogram definitions for model training, evaluation and future livestock behaviour research. Cattle-CLIP was evaluated under supervised and few-shot learning settings, with particular emphasis on data-constrained behaviour recognition. In supervised experiments, the model achieved 96.1% accuracy across six behaviours and showed high precision and recall for feeding, drinking and standing-ruminating. It also maintained promising few-shot performance with limited labelled data, demonstrating the potential of multimodal learning for cattle behaviour recognition in data-constrained farm environments.</p></details> |  |
| **[Pathway-Structured Privileged Distillation for Deployable Computational Pathology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02877v3)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Integrating transcriptomics and histopathology can improve cancer risk modelling, yet practical use is constrained by the limited availability of RNA profiling in routine settings. Here we introduce Mixture of Pathway Experts (MoPE), a knowledge-distillation framework that reframes multimodal learning as privileged distillation for histology-only inference. MoPE is motivated by the partial observability between RNA profiles and whole-slide images: histology can capture morphology-linked consequences of certain molecular programmes, but cannot be expected to reconstruct the full transcriptomic state. MoPE encodes RNA-derived pathways and transfers the molecular supervision to pathway-indexed pathology experts through memory-usage alignment. Across diverse public benchmarks and two independent breast cancer cohorts, MoPE consistently improved WSI-only inference performance relative to baseline methods. Pathway-usage analyses and human-audited visual inspection provide bounded inspection of model behaviour and candidate morphology-linked readouts. These results support pathway-structured privileged distillation as a promising route to using molecular information during training while preserving RNA-free inference.</p></details> |  |
| **[CIIR - Class-Indexed Interface Representation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23151v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal learning commonly assumes paired or synchronized observations, yet independently collected datasets rarely provide such correspondence. We introduce CIIR, a modular encoder--decoder framework that connects heterogeneous datasets through a class-indexed address space while preserving each dataset's native input format and output module. A known local index selects address $k$, and an input-dependent 128-dimensional address representation Z_k(x) is written to that address. To demonstrate CIIR, we learn this address space from handwritten digits, clothing images, spoken-digit spectrograms, and hand signs without synchronized or instance-matched samples. Newly trained label-free receivers recover the requested addresses with 94.7% macro-average Address Realization Accuracy. We then attach the unseen SVHN dataset after freezing the base address space and train only lightweight adapters, achieving 90.2% MNIST>SVHN and 82.3% SVHN>MNIST end-to-end Address Transfer Accuracy without target-side decoder labels. To demonstrate generalizability of CIIR, we reuse the ten addresses as code elements in collision-free arithmetic and hierarchical address codes for all 100 CIFAR-100 classes and evaluate direct arithmetic-code realization through the existing receivers. These results establish CIIR as a compact interface for modular cross-dataset communication and post-hoc dataset attachment without exhaustive paired multimodal supervision.</p></details> | 17 pages, 6 figures |
| **[TWIN-MoE: Two-stage Intra-Expert Imputation Network for Incomplete Multimodal Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24014v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) approaches address missing modalities either by routing each missingness pattern to a dedicated expert, which scales poorly as the number of modalities grows, or by imputing the missing modality from representations contributed by multiple experts, which requires a costly retrieval mechanism. We show that routing induces expert-conditioned representation neighborhoods and derive an imputation-error bound controlled by their local feature dispersion. When these expert-conditioned neighborhoods are more compact than the global representation space, intra-expert aggregation admits a tighter worst-case reconstruction bound than unconstrained global aggregation. Guided by this analysis, we propose TWIN, a two-stage intra-expert imputation framework. Pre-imputation generates a context-conditioned estimate in the encoder embedding space and radially soft-clips it using modality-specific statistics maintained in an observed FIFO queue, this ensures that the pre-imputed modality stays in empirically calibrated region of the target modality space. Post-imputation refines the missing representation after MoE by aggregating expert-conditioned observed representations within the same sample. Across four missing-modality benchmarks under natural, random, and asymmetric dropout protocols, TWIN achieves state-of-the-art performance while using substantially fewer parameters than competing MoE-based imputation methods.</p></details> |  |
| **[When to Align, When to Predict: A Phase Diagram for Multimodal Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.11190v4)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Cross-modal alignment (CA) and cross-modal prediction (CP) are the dominant paradigms for multimodal representation learning, yet there is no systematic understanding of when each succeeds and when each fails, a gap that leaves practitioners, especially in scientific domains with heterogeneous instruments and multiple levels of measurement, unable to diagnose why standard methods underperform the best single modality. We study both objectives under a spiked signal-plus-noise model with structured cross-modal nuisance correlation, the ingredient that breaks the classical recovery guarantees, and derive separation ratios that expose complementary failure modes: alignment whitens each modality and fails when nuisance is strongly correlated across views; prediction encodes whatever is cross-predictable through a one-sided whitening, with recovery governed by source-modality quality. The resulting phase diagram partitions multimodal problems into four regimes --- Both, CA only, CP only, and Neither --- refined by a recovery count that separates partial recovery from complete failure. We present a data-driven procedure to locate real-world datasets in this diagram using a small labeled subsample, identifying the preferred objective and prediction direction before any cross-modal training, and identifying when no objective in the CA/CP family can improve on the stronger modality alone. Experiments on synthetic data, stereo-vision benchmarks, image--caption pairs, and two real scientific domains, astronomy and single-cell multi-omics, validate the predictions in the nonlinear regime, including both faces of the Neither regime. Code to reproduce the results is available at https://github.com/IlayMalinyak/mm_align_vs_pred.</p></details> |  |
| **[Task-Centric Multimodal Learning for Cognitive Impairment Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20906v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Dementia affects millions of older adults worldwide, yet early detection remains difficult outside clinical settings. Traditional models primarily rely on text or voice-based responses, capturing only a limited view of human cognition despite the inherently multimodal nature of human expression. This work proposes a contactless, multimodal screening framework that leverages video, audio, and text signals collected during structured voice-based interactions with Amazon Alexa. By incorporating these modalities, our approach captures a more holistic representation of human behavior encompassing speech patterns, vocal characteristics, facial expressions, and linguistic structure, which may enable more accurate detection of cognitive decline. Different participants completed three conversational tasks designed to probe spontaneous speech, comprehension, and recall. We pursued three objectives: (1) evaluate which of the three Alexa tasks provides the most informative cognitive signals, (2) compare the effectiveness of unimodal models, and (3) assess the benefits of multimodal fusion models. Task-level analysis shows that the video model achieves its best performance on Command-Reading, with an accuracy of 0.65 (AUC 0.69), while the audio model performs best on Question-Answering, reaching an accuracy of 0.67 (AUC 0.73). In contrast, the multimodal model demonstrates consistently stable performance across tasks, achieving an accuracy of 0.76 (AUC 0.75) on Command-Reading and 0.76 (AUC 0.73) on Question-Answering. These findings demonstrate that multimodal behavioral cues captured through naturalistic interactions can reliably distinguish MCI from healthy aging, highlighting the limitations of traditional diagnostic paradigms and supporting the shift toward multimodal, real-world cognitive assessment frameworks.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted for publication at the 2026 IEEE Global Communications Conference (GLOBECOM)</p></details> |
| **[Instruction Mining From Images: Constructing a Synthetic Dataset for Multimodal Learning ILSP DCVLR Competition Submission](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19932v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This work presents a compact and reproducible pipeline for synthesizing and curating image-based instruction-response pairs for DCVLR. We draw from the Cauldron collection and use Magpie-style instruction and response synthesis with Qwen2.5-VL-7B Instruct to create concise, image-conditioned instruction pairs. The pipeline consists of instruction synthesis, conditioned on input images and controlled via a system prompt, sanitization, tagging, and filtering of the instructions based on the score obtained by a reward model (DeBERTa-v3). We retain samples with positive scores to maximize the quality of the generated instructions. We observe that score distributions have a median and mean reward score of ~ 1.53 and ~ 1.75 respectively, indicating that most generated instructions are of good quality. The resulting dataset contains diverse instructions in the math, spatial, and social reasoning domains.</p></details> |  |
| **[Towards Modality-Agnostic Seizure Classification: A Unified Deep Learning Framework for Heterogeneous EEG and Intracranial EEG Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16596v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Seizure prediction and detection, essential for clinical treatment of epilepsy, remain the crucial task of this neurological disease. EEG and iEEG are widely used to analyze epilepsy due to its capability of capturing discriminative brain activities at high time resolution. However, existing works generally analyze either EEG or iEEG seizure activity separately, where EEG analysis has been well-researched, while more and more works consider iEEG for its higher specificity at the spatial level and signal quality. Currently, most of the existing seizure prediction systems are single-modal in nature, which limits its application to a variety of clinical conditions. Therefore, a common framework to analyze seizures with both EEG and iEEG signals needs to be developed. This paper presents a new framework of multimodal seizure classification integrating the heterogeneous EEG and iEEG recordings from different subjects and sources. A specific framework, called Unified Multimodal Signal Harmonization (UMSH), is developed for harmonizing signals with varied sampling rates and recording conditions. Moreover, to handle the extremely class-imbalanced problem in the data sets, we use a Conditional Generative Adversarial Network (cGAN) based data augmentation technique to generate synthetic samples to balance the class distribution. The proposed framework uses the EEGNet-Vision Transformer (EEGNet-ViT) as the feature extraction and classification network to learn the local discriminative patterns as well as global contextual information in the multimodal seizure data. Experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed framework for robust seizure classification, emphasizing the potential of integrated EEG-iEEG multimodal learning in advancing next-generation epilepsy monitoring and clinical decision-support systems.</p></details> |  |
| **[More Than Seeing: Vision as Scalable Anchors in Native Multimodal Pre-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19443v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Native multimodal pre-training trains a single model from scratch on text and multimodal data, enabling it to understand both language and visual inputs. However, how visual signals shape scaling behavior and support capabilities beyond visual understanding remains unclear. We investigate this question through controlled visual-content ablations across model sizes and training budgets. Specifically, we replace image embeddings with content-free padding token embeddings. Retaining visual content improves text prediction on image-text examples without materially changing the lowest text-only loss at a given compute budget, the optimal balance of model size and training data, or aggregate language scores. The relative multimodal loss benefit grows approximately as a power law of compute. To test benefits beyond understanding images, we evaluate spatial reasoning from text descriptions alone. Both conditions improve with model and data scaling. During training of the largest model, visual gains are most sustained in localization, navigation, and symbolic planning, which involve maintaining object configurations or composing spatial updates; gains in spatial relations, geometry, and physical commonsense are less consistent. These findings identify visual content as a scalable anchor for multimodal learning, with benefits that extend beyond visual inputs while preserving aggregate language performance.</p></details> |  |
| **[Do More Modalities Always Help? A Geometric Perspective on Missing-Modality Robustness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04792v2)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Missing modality remains a longstanding challenge in multimodal learning. Existing methods typically address this issue through modality recovery or adaptive strategies. However, they overlook models' internal cross-modal dependencies formed during multimodal training, which later impair robustness. We systematically characterize a counterintuitive deployment-time failure mode: models trained on full modalities can underperform unimodal models when one modality is missing at inference time. This pattern appears across diverse architectures, such as fusion models, CLIP-style two-tower models, and vision-language models. We show that such degradation is closely associated with learned cross-modal dependencies in the principal parameter subspaces. Multimodal training induces structured rotations of these subspaces, particularly in cross-modal interaction layers. These rotations are associated with reduced task-aligned margins and larger task-aware representation harm under missing-modality inputs. We propose Geodesic Unlearning (GU), a lightweight parameter-editing method that leverages Grassmannian subspace geometry for structured subspace correction to improve missing-modality robustness. It rotates the principal input subspace toward a unimodal reference along a geodesic path. We prove that this correction minimizes the distance to the reference within a fixed subspace-distance budget. Experiments across architectures and datasets show that GU improves performance under missing-modality inference while preserving full-modality accuracy, outperforming strong missing-modality robustness baselines. These findings support a geometric view of deployment-time missing-modality degradation and suggest localized subspace editing as a practical route for robustness correction.</p></details> | <details><summary>NeurI...</summary><p>NeurIPS 2026 Main Conference</p></details> |
| **[SYNPRED: A Synergistic Approach to Multimodal Learning for Clinical Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18110v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal medical data captures complementary aspects of disease. It provides a comprehensive basis for diagnosis and the identification of biological associations linked to progression or treatment response. Established contrastive learning approaches exploit shared information across modalities, but fall short in exploiting complementary, modality-specific signals relevant for a task. Here, we propose a synergistic multimodal variational autoencoder (VAE) framework that separates task-relevant unimodal and multimodal information from instance-specific variation task-irrelevant, allowing complementary signals across modalities to be leveraged for prediction. The method yields benefits even if modalities are missing at test time. We evaluate the method on a treatment response prediction task using medical imaging and RNA data. Empirical comparison with alignment-based and multimodal VAE state-of-the-art approaches demonstrates improved multimodal prediction performance, and consistent benefits for prediction from a single modality after multimodal training. The full code can be found here: https://github.com/cirmuw/SYNPRED.</p></details> |  |
| **[Beyond Forced Modality Balance: Intrinsic Information Budgets for Multimodal Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.17347v2)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal models often converge to a dominant-modality solution, in which a stronger, faster-converging modality overshadows weaker ones. This modality imbalance causes suboptimal performance. Existing methods attempt to balance different modalities by reweighting gradients or losses. However, they overlook the fact that each modality has finite information capacity. In this work, we propose IIBalance, a multimodal learning framework that aligns the modality contributions with Intrinsic Information Budgets (IIB). We propose a task-grounded estimator of each modality's IIB, transforming its capacity into a global prior over modality contributions. Anchored by the highest-budget modality, we design a prototype-based relative alignment mechanism that corrects semantic drift only when weaker modalities deviate from their budgeted potential, rather than forcing imitation. During inference, we propose a probabilistic gating module that integrates the global budgets with sample-level uncertainty to generate calibrated fusion weights. Experiments on three representative benchmarks demonstrate that IIBalance consistently outperforms state-of-the-art balancing methods and achieves better utilization of complementary modality cues. Our code is available at: https://github.com/XiongZechang/IIBalance.</p></details> | <details><summary>6 pag...</summary><p>6 pages, 4 figures, paper accepted by ICME 2026</p></details> |
| **[MODAIC: Coordinated Asynchrony for Decentralized Multimodal Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16987v1)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal decentralized learning enables collaboration across heterogeneous edge devices without sharing raw data, but differing modality sets create coupled computation and communication bottlenecks. Multimodal agents support cross-modal alignment while training more model components and sharing bandwidth across multiple modality overlays. Under bandwidth limits, these agents can become hot spots: transfers miss aggregation windows, and their updates become stale or underrepresented, degrading worst-agent accuracy. Asynchrony removes global blocking but does not by itself coordinate local cross-modal interaction with timely peer-update exchange. We present MODAIC (Modality-Decoupled Asynchrony with Interleaved Coordination), a wait-free system that coordinates local modality progress, peer-to-peer update delivery, and aggregation under heterogeneous compute and bandwidth. Hierarchical asynchrony lets modalities progress independently while enforcing reciprocal feature exchange; load-aware update fetching overlaps training with communication, limits concurrent serving load, and reuses late arrivals; and consensus-aware peer selection uses lightweight metadata to account for update availability, peer participation, and loss similarity without active probing. On AVE at 200 Mbps per agent, it improves multimodal worst-agent accuracy by 11.0 percentage points at 1000 seconds over the strongest baseline. | **[Metric-Driven Reinforcement Learning for Object Hallucination Reduction in LVLM-Based Image Captioning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14976v1)** | 2026-10-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs) have excelled in joint visual and language understanding, particularly in image captioning. This survey emphasizes two key aspects: on one hand, multimodal learning enables contextual semantic understanding for improved facial state reasoning and leverages interpretable language generation to enhance model explainability; on the other hand, multi-task learning allows simultaneous analysis of expressions, action units (AUs), and face-based soft biometrics (e.g., age, gender), effectively capturing fine-grained expressions and improving cross-scene generalization. The survey aims to provide an up-to-date overview of the literature and to highlight future research directions for multimodal, interpretable, and multi-task adaptive facial state analysis.</p></details> |  |
| **[Explainable Multimodal Deep Learning Integrating Imaging and Clinical Data for Oral Potentially Malignant Disorder Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04512v1)** | 2026-09-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Oral potentially malignant disorders (OPMDs) are critical precursors to oral cancer, yet clinical detection remains challenging because of substantial phenotypic heterogeneity and overlap with benign conditions. | **[A Survey on Self-Improving Test-Time Intelligence: Feedback-Driven Adapting, Learning, and Scaling at Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01679v1)** | 2026-09-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The ability of AI systems to improve their behavior during deployment is becoming increasingly important. However, these directions are often studied in separate communities with different terminology, making their connections harder to see. In this survey, we present feedback-driven Test-Time Intelligence (TTI) as a unified perspective for understanding such deployment-time improvement. We use this view to relate test-time adaptation, test-time learning, and test-time scaling, highlighting both their distinctions and their growing overlap in hybrid systems. This unified framework helps connect previously fragmented ideas and provides a clearer conceptual foundation for studying inference-time self-improvement. We review major methodological paradigms, representative applications, and open challenges across vision, language, multimodal learning, generative models, robotics, and healthcare. Our goal is to provide a coherent foundation and research roadmap for the study of self-improving AI systems at test time.</p></details> | <details><summary>accep...</summary><p>accepted by Machine Intelligence Research</p></details> |
| **[Semantics at an Angle: When Cosine Similarity Works Until It Doesn't](https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.16318v4)** | 2026-08-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Cosine similarity is a standard comparison rule for learned representations in information retrieval, natural language processing, computer vision, and multimodal learning. Its popularity is well founded: it removes positive radial scale, is computationally convenient, and often matches objectives that train normalized embeddings. These same properties also delimit what cosine can express. Normalization discards radial variation; anisotropic representations can compress angular contrast; high-dimensional neighborhoods can develop hubs; and a symmetric, uncalibrated score may be mismatched to the relation of interest. This article offers a selective review organized around a simple principle: the usefulness of cosine similarity depends jointly on the learned representation, any normalization or post-processing, the scoring rule, and the downstream decision. We derive the main geometric identities, distinguish failure mechanisms that are often conflated, review representative evidence about embedding norms, and describe objective-matched, geometry-aware, hubness-aware, norm-aware, and learned alternatives. The central conclusion is conditional rather than adversarial: cosine's positive-scale invariance is justified when radial variation is nuisance or fixed by the representation contract, but its angular geometry and downstream decision must still be validated.</p></details> |  |

Rather than enforcing rigid cross-modal alignment, \regName follows a bounded-agreement principle: preserve modality-specific diversity while softly constraining only the portion of paired cross-modal drift that exceeds an admissible agreement band. Operationally, \regName combines a dispersion term that mitigates spectral concentration with an agreement-band anchoring term that controls excessive paired drift, requiring no architectural modification or inference-time overhead. Experiments across audio-visual, image-text, and RF-based benchmarks show that \regName consistently improves multimodal performance and often strengthens unimodal representations. | **[PANDA - Prototype-Anchored Alignment for Partially Unpaired Multimodal Learning, with Applications to Alzheimers MRI and TCGA Pathology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.25970v1)** | 2026-08-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal medical prediction often faces incomplete pairing: auxiliary modalities with complementary signal are available for only a subset of subjects (or none) and cannot be assumed at deployment. | **[Hyperbolic Multimodal Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.09572v1)** | 2026-08-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Hyperbolic geometry has recently emerged as a powerful representation space for multimodal learning, as it naturally captures hierarchical semantic structure across modalities. We further show that forgetting in hyperbolic continual learning involves both semantic relation drift and hierarchy-related distortion, motivating preservation of both cross-modal relational structure and hierarchical geometry. | **[How Much Reconstruction Does Quantum Machine Learning Need? We do not claim advantage over classical machine learning - consistent with recent benchmarking, quantum offers no accuracy edge on these datasets. ## Representation Learning
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[Implicit Feature Reweighting for Label-Free Graph Generalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29571v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Graph-level out-of-distribution (OOD) generalization is critical for deploying graph neural networks in real-world applications where distribution shifts are inevitable. Existing approaches typically require class labels during representation learning to decide which substructures are predictive, limiting their applicability. Our method operates in two stages: (1) a variational graph autoencoder is warm-started for graph reconstruction; and (2) an adversarial confusion mechanism forces the encoder to produce representations from which a discriminator cannot distinguish environments, while preserving graph-reconstruction fidelity. We provide theoretical analysis showing that environment confusion contracts environment-discriminative directions in the representation space, which, under regularized downstream classifiers, is equivalent to imposing a stronger directional penalty on environment-associated features. This feature-reweighting mechanism reduces reliance on spurious correlations without erasing information, offering a label-free, continuous relaxation of last-layer reweighting methods. Experiments on a controlled synthetic datasets and the DrugOOD datasets show that SCARCE achieves state-of-the-art OOD generalization among label-free methods, using no class labels during representation learning.</p></details> | <details><summary>We pr...</summary><p>We propose an implicit feature reweighting method to enhance graph-level OOD generalization without class labels</p></details> |

| **[BrainPro: Process-conditioned Pretraining with Topographic Retrieval for Large EEG Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.22050v3)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>EEG foundation models are pretrained on heterogeneous corpora, but two challenges remain underexplored: (1) experimental protocols provide mental-process cues that are rarely used in masked pretraining, and (2) electrode montages vary across datasets, making spatial representations montage-dependent. | **[Mixture-of-Control: State-Aware Fine-Tuning for Transformer-based Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.31397v2)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>State-based fine-tuning has emerged as a compelling alternative to weight-based adaptation for transformers, updating lightweight controls into states rather than model weights, offering substantial memory savings while retaining parameter efficiency. | **[Touch-JEPA: Tactile Representation Learning from Hand-Object Video with Limited Tactile Supervision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28519v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Understanding contact and tactile magnitude in hand-object interaction is a key step toward physically intelligent systems, yet learning such representations is limited by the cost and restricted coverage of tactile measurements. | **[Exploring Temporal Context-Aware Physical Grounding in Latent World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28483v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Learning latent dynamics is fundamental to building action-conditioned world models for planning. To align this causal state evolution with observable dynamics, we introduce Unified Motion Supervision. Rather than treating motion as an independent objective, UMS leverages geometry-aware predictions from the camera and depth heads to construct a unified forward motion field that is consistent with the underlying 3D structure. Stage II introduces a mutual report refinement mechanism that identifies diagnostic inconsistencies within prior reports and iteratively rectifies both prior and current reports through error-sensitive feedback. Experiments on the Longitudinal-MIMIC dataset demonstrate that TIM surpasses existing single-image and longitudinal baselines, achieving new state-of-the-art performance across both language and clinical metrics. Code is available at https://github.com/yihengd/TIM.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by CVPR 2026</p></details> |

D.2</p></details> |

| **[From Prediction to Action: Population Health World Model for Digital Medicine](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24662v1)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Digital medicine now captures data that traces individual health trajectories and reveals population health dynamics through electronic health records, claims, remote monitoring, wearable devices, mobile devices, social sensing data, medical images, etc. But much of its modeling still centers on task-specific, outcome-specific, and prediction-specific estimation. The next frontier is not simply better prediction, but action in an evolving health world shaped by multiple decision-makers, heterogeneous populations, resource constraints, and feedback in care delivery. This is why digital medicine now needs world models: not only to estimate what will happen, but to represent how actions change what can happen next -- whom to prioritize, how to allocate scarce resources, how to adapt care across subgroups, and how interventions reshape future health trajectories and population dynamics. We call such a model population health world model (PHWM): a more foundational representation of an evolving health world in which health states, individual trajectories, population health dynamics, interventions, constraints, and feedback are intrinsically coupled. We argue that addressing these gaps requires three minimal capabilities: representation learning under action-dependent observation, multi-scale intervention intelligence, and validation with explicit decision boundaries, while highlighting the importance of social, contextual, and public-health sensing data for action-oriented digital medicine.</p></details> |  |

## Causal Inference
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[Proximal Balancing for Causal Effect Estimation under Unmeasured Confounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40051v2)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Estimating causal effects from observational data is central to science and policy, but the effects are not identified when confounders are unmeasured. Proximal causal inference addresses this problem with proxies of the unmeasured confounders. However, existing proxy-based approaches either designate proxy roles and solve an inverse problem, which is ill-posed and hard to estimate with high-dimensional proxies, or use a latent-variable model, which assumes that the learned latent variable matches the hidden confounder and leaves bias when it does not. To address these challenges, we introduce proximal balancing. It carries the classical idea of covariate balancing to confounders that are observed only through proxies: it learns a low-dimensional summary of the covariates and proxies that makes the treatment groups comparable, and then adjusts for this summary. It needs no designated proxy roles, inverse problem, or latent model. We give identification theory, finite-sample guarantees, and a practical algorithm, PROBE. We demonstrate the method on low-dimensional, high-dimensional, and image proxies and on real-world data.</p></details> | <details><summary>59 pa...</summary><p>59 pages. Code: https://github.com/CausalDataScience/proximal-balancing</p></details> |
| **[Estimand frameworks for causal inference in clinical research and regulatory decision making](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28983v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Causal inference for clinical and regulatory decision making requires a precise definition of the causal estimand, that is, what we want to estimate. Three frameworks are commonly used to describe causal estimands: PICO and its extensions, the target trial framework, and the E9(R1) Addendum of the International Council for Harmonisation (ICH). Because these frameworks were developed by different scientific communities, they operate at different levels of detail, emphasize different aspects of the causal estimand, and use different terminology for closely related concepts. Here we compare the three frameworks and translate across their terminologies. PICO is a compact framework that helps jumpstart a conversation about the causal question, but it does not encode sufficient information to fully characterize a causal estimand. The target trial framework characterizes the causal estimand precisely by specifying the components of the protocol of the randomized trial that would answer the causal question: eligibility criteria, treatment strategies, assignment, outcome, start and end of follow-up, and causal contrast. E9(R1) starts from intercurrent events and lists strategies to address them, some of which describe estimators rather than estimands, or imply estimands that are not relevant to decision makers or cannot be estimated under reasonable assumptions. We argue that the target trial framework subsumes the other two, and we provide a map of the correspondences across the three frameworks.</p></details> |  |

As interest in causal discovery builds in fields such as ecology, public health, and environmental sciences, where data are regularly collected with spatial and temporal structures, approaches must evolve to manage autocorrelation and complex confounding. As it stands, the few proposed causal discovery algorithms for spatiotemporal data require summarizing across locations, ignore spatial autocorrelation, and/or scale poorly to high dimensions. Here, we introduce our framework to extend time-series causal discovery to systems with spatial structure. Building upon work on causal discovery across contexts and methods for handling spatial confounding in causal effect estimation, we prove the soundness and completeness of our extension in the large sample limit. Using tensor-product B-spline bases, the resulting algorithm expresses inference through numerical quadrature and linear algebra without explicitly constructing conditional distributions. We further characterize the semiparametric efficiency bound, construct an efficient estimator, and establish necessary and sufficient conditions under which additional measurements yield strict efficiency gains. We illustrate our approach through an application to the Hajj visa lottery. Our framework provides practical guidance for measurement design, identification, and efficient causal estimation with latent outcomes.</p></details> |  |

| **[Where to weight? However, when the observational data are drawn from a complex survey, the existing literature does not provide sufficient guidance on the proper use of survey weights in matching strategies for population-level causal inference. We develop weighted double score matching (WDSM) that provides a unified framework for incorporating survey weights throughout the score estimation, matching, and outcome analysis under both retrospective (informative) and prospective (non-informative) sampling designs. We establish asymptotic normality of the resultant effect estimates under both known and estimated scores and develop a linearization-based bootstrap procedure for variance estimation that preserves the survey-weighted matching structure. Simulation studies show that WDSM substantially reduces bias relative to unweighted matching when either the propensity score model or the prognostic score models are correctly specified. Under correct specification, WDSM also improves efficiency and confidence interval coverage relative to survey-weighted propensity score matching, with particularly pronounced gains under poor overlap. Applications to early marijuana use and mental health in the National Survey on Drug Use and Health and to sleep duration and glycemic control in the National Health and Nutrition Examination Survey illustrate the practical value of WDSM for population-level inference. | **[Inverse Confounding Analysis: An Exact Method for Quantifying the Significance of Confounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11991v5)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The presence of unmeasured confounding factors during the collection of observational data may lead to biased estimates of the effect of an exposure on an outcome. Existing sensitivity analyses generally focus on worst-case bounds. We propose an exact method for quantifying the significance of confounding, defined here in terms of the complete range of analytical estimates of the stratification-based Risk Ratio over the set of all joint distributions compatible with the observed characteristics. We refer to the proposed method as Inverse Confounding Analysis (ICA). The proposed ICA method extends the widely used E-value approach but, in contrast to it, does not restrict the analysis to a worst-case lower bound. Instead, it provides exact estimates over the entire set of admissible configurations. This requires several additional input parameters, namely the frequencies of the exposure, the confounder, and the outcome. The ICA method is based on an inverse problem: reconstructing the set of admissible joint distributions from specified frequencies and pairwise associations. We formulate this reconstruction problem as a system of nonlinear equations and obtain an analytical solution. Surprisingly, the complete solution set can be parameterized linearly by a single free parameter. The corresponding stratification-based Risk Ratio is then represented as a fractional-linear function of this parameter. | **[Which Covariates to Adjust for? | **[What Social Media Teaches Us About Accumulative AI Risks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24006v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Prominent AI safety discussions emphasize existential threats and catastrophic events, such as AI-enabled nuclear conflict or engineered biological outbreaks. Drawing on lessons from social media, we argue that safety assessments must also address accumulative harms that develop through repeated use, recurring decisions, and feedback across interconnected deployments. Repeated reassurance, selective exposure, recurring decision biases, and growing personal profiles may gradually narrow perspectives, weaken independent judgment, or enable exploitative influence without a single clearly harmful event. We advance the Trajectory Neglect Thesis: a focus on vivid catastrophic events and identifiable incidents risks overlooking less visible harms that accumulate through the widespread daily use of AI. Social media research motivates this position by showing how reciprocal media effects, social reinforcement, and individual susceptibility shape outcomes beyond individual encounters. We examine how related mechanisms may operate in AI systems, using conversational AI as a concrete setting where generation, semantic inference, and persistent memory may tighten feedback loops. Sycophancy, manipulation, and privacy loss provide a case study of how these risks may interact and accumulate. This comparison identifies mechanisms to investigate rather than assuming that repeated AI use is harmful. We therefore call for evaluation of cumulative patterns of use and deployment, individual and societal outcomes, careful causal inference, and proportionate safeguards that preserve useful assistance and informed user choice.</p></details> | <details><summary>Camer...</summary><p>Camera-ready version for CogMI 2026 vision track</p></details> |

| **[Graph Dirichlet Energy for Fitting-Free Bivariate Causal Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21892v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We propose the Graph Dirichlet Energy Causal Inference (GDECI) criterion for distinguishing cause from effect in bivariate settings. For a candidate direction $X\to Y$, we construct a kernel similarity graph from $X$ and treat $Y$ as a graph signal. The score is the Dirichlet energy of this signal, induced by the unnormalized graph Laplacian. The reverse score swaps the variables, and the lower-energy direction is inferred as causal. The method requires neither regression fitting nor independence testing. After the empirical probability integral transform, its decisions are invariant under strictly monotone transformations of either variable, and the graph operations reduce to convolutions, allowing exact evaluation in $O(n\log n)$ time and $O(n)$ memory. At the population level, GDECI compares degree-weighted Bayes squared prediction errors plus a non-negative smoothness term of the regression function. As the bandwidth tends to zero, the weights become uniform and the smoothness term vanishes, reducing the criterion to a RECI-type comparison of expected conditional variances in copula space. Empirically, we compare GDECI with QCCD, GRCI, CAM, IGCI (two variants), RESIT, LOCI, QPE-k, and RECI (two variants), in accuracy on 1899 cause--effect pairs and in inference speed. GDECI ranked fifth of eleven methods in macro-averaged accuracy on 12 synthetic suites ($0.776$). On the real-world Tuebingen pairs, it ranked third in unweighted accuracy ($0.684$) and fifth in weighted accuracy ($0.678$). It was also the fastest of the compared methods up to $n\approx10^{4}$. We present ExecWeave, a local-first system that materializes provider semantics and independently observed runtime events into a typed execution graph. An optional command-to-process correlator emits a non-causal inferred bridge only when its bounded search yields one compatible observed process; uniqueness within this observed candidate set is not a guarantee of causal correctness. OS-observed file and network effects acquire a semantic tool or agent principal only through an observed process plus a justified command-to-process association; session-correlated file-system events do not by themselves provide that principal. More broadly, this work provides a unifying foundation for medical decision-analytical modelling and causal inference, making explicit the potential for decision-analytical model bias and the role of causal assumptions contributing to it. | **[CausalReasoningBenchmark: A Real-World Benchmark for Disentangled Evaluation of Causal Identification and Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.20571v3)** | 2026-09-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Many benchmarks for automated causal inference evaluate a system's performance based on a single numerical output, such as an Average Treatment Effect (ATE). | **[LongZero: Enhancing Long-Context Reasoning in LLMs from Zero Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30132v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reinforcement learning (RL) is an effective way to strengthen the long-context reasoning of large language models (LLMs), but it needs training data that are long, diverse, challenging, and verifiable. Recently, RL from zero data, in which a model learns from tasks it proposes itself, has achieved excellent performance, but existing methods target short-context tasks. Therefore, we propose $\textbf{LongZero}$, a method that lets an LLM improve its own long-context reasoning without any external data. Instead of writing questions and answers over existing documents, LongZero first has the model synthesize the question and answer with its supporting evidence in a compact structured form, and only then separately expands the evidence into a long multi-document context. LongZero combines these self-written multi-hop reasoning tasks with self-written multi-round retrieval tasks and trains the model with GRPO. We evaluate LongZero on two LLMs across five long-context reasoning benchmarks and compare it with RL on data synthesized from external corpora. Experimental results show that LongZero can generalize to real tasks, and outperforms both baselines, suggesting a viable and promising path to improving long-context reasoning of LLMs.</p></details> | <details><summary>Code ...</summary><p>Code and data are available at https://github.com/zhennan1/LongZero</p></details> |
| **[Environment Duels: A Microcosm of LLM Co-Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30116v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Through a tangle of real-world interactions, from synthetic training pipelines to data shared on the open web, frontier LLMs absorb what their predecessors and competitors create. Each model writes environments, each with a privileged hint, aiming for tasks that capable solvers fail without the hint and pass with it. Across nine frontier LLMs, solving and authoring prove to be distinct skills: the strongest solvers are not the strongest authors, while smaller models learn more from how others played and write the hardest environments and the most useful hints. Beyond the scores, the pairwise interactions form a microcosm of how models affect one another. Head-to-head outcomes are transitive and so yield a stable ranking; independent authors converge on the same designs; and in a reinforcement-learning study with Qwen3.8-27B, the learner's hint lift on an author's environments tracks how much training on those environments improves it on held-out reasoning and agentic benchmarks, whether the environments are its own or another model's. Each of these patterns has a counterpart in how models shape one another outside the duel. By staging these interactions under controlled conditions, Environment Duels offers a window onto how LLMs teach, challenge and learn from one another. We introduce Quant-Arena, a benchmark and public leaderboard for end-to-end quantitative research on real A-share market data. | **[Circuits of LLM Classifiers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30076v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Language models make it easy to build classifiers by simply prompting a model with the sample to classify. Because a generated harness may exclude inputs or paths that trigger real bugs, Evident restricts how the harness initializes inputs and validates it using the value flow reported by the original detector. | **[EPIC: Rethinking Trust Regions for Autoregressive LLM Policies via Exact-KL Prefix Importance-Ratio Clipping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29999v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reinforcement learning (RL) based on PPO-style clipping has become an effective approach to improving the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs). A versioned public-operator correction yields 923 scorable records, 99 with insufficient valid dates, and 25 unsupported, without modifying the other 990 prior diagnostics. Among scorable records, 671 have positive and 252 negative signed mean rank-IC. Accepted for poster presentation at the AI4F Workshop at ACM ICAIF 2026 (non-archival); attendance confirmation pending</p></details> |

| **[CertMoE: Robustness Certification for Mixture-of-Experts LLMs via Routing-Aware Smoothing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29395v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Provable robustness is a cornerstone of trustworthy machine learning, aiming to guarantee model predictions under adversarial perturbations. This hierarchical certification procedure yields a joint probabilistic guarantee over routing containment and sub-network robustness, providing sufficient conditions under which the original prediction remains invariant. Extensive experiments across recent MoE LLMs demonstrate that dynamic routing makes certificates issued by existing methods frequently violated, while \textsc{CertMoE} reduces the empirical violation rate with routing-aware guarantees, e.g., from more than $30\%$ to $0$, yielding certificates that more faithfully reflect the robustness of MoE models.</p></details> | 12 pages |

| **[From Atomic to Agentic: Towards Interpretable Evaluation of LLMs' Agentic Mathematical Capabilities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26950v2)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) are evolving from performing end-to-end mathematical reasoning to integrating agentic intelligence. This outcome-oriented evaluation provides limited diagnostic value for identifying process-level failures or rigorous logic, failing to guide the transformation of LLMs into robust agents. To bridge this gap, we present a process-level benchmark designed to evaluate the inherent agentic mathematical reasoning abilities of LLMs. Our framework aligns problem-solving agentic behaviors with a structured taxonomy of reusable mathematical atomic capabilities. We design a comprehensive suite of planning, action, and feedback tasks across both textual and multimodal contexts, supported by an automated pipeline that synthesizes high-quality trajectories and produces fine-grained annotations via controlled LLM rewriting. Experiments reveal that models with similar end-to-end accuracy can exhibit markedly different agentic capability profiles. This demonstrates that process-level evaluation is crucial for interpreting the true potential of LLMs and guiding the development of next-generation mathematical agents.</p></details> | EMNLP 2026 |
| **[Does Every Instance Count Equally? Order Effects in LLM Multi-Instance Processing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29057v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Users increasingly rely on large language models (LLMs) to analyse collections of independent inputs, such as product reviews or social media posts, and return a single aggregated answer. This setting requires multi-instance processing (MIP), where a model combines information from multiple instances into one prediction. We study a task in which the model must infer a binary label for each instance, and identify the most frequent label among the full instance set. The correct answer should be invariant to instance order. By evaluating fifteen LLMs across five datasets and seven instance order patterns, with each instance set held fixed across patterns, we find substantial order sensitivity. Accuracy can differ by about 90 percentage points between the best and worst order patterns, with models generally more accurate when majority-label instances appear at the start than when they appear in the middle or at the end. For subsets of models, available reasoning traces and gradient-based attribution reveal uneven emphasis across positions. Traces disproportionately mention early instances, while attribution scores are generally higher near the beginning and end than in the middle. These results show that instance order can systematically bias aggregated predictions, highlighting the need to evaluate MIP across different input arrangements.</p></details> |  |
| **[AutoFraming: Synthesizing Dynamic Frames of C Code with LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29046v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) have shown considerable promise in synthesizing formal specifications from source code. To address this limitation, we propose an LLM-based approach for synthesizing ACSL frame specifications alongside functional contracts from C code. Our approach analyzes code property graphs (CPGs) to extract frame-relevant information from the input code and leverages this information to guide the LLM in generating accurate frame conditions. The resulting specifications capture both region-separation constraints and data dependencies that govern variable modifications. Experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of our approach. On a benchmark of 84 ACSL-compliant functions from non-trivial real-world systems, our method successfully synthesizes error-free frame specifications for 92.9% of the functions, representing a 179% relative improvement over state-of-the-art techniques. On a second benchmark consisting of 51 functions, the success rate increases from 80.4% to 96.1%. Furthermore, when integrated as an auxiliary component into existing LLM-based ACSL-synthesis frameworks, our approach improves frame-specification generation performance by up to 61.9%.</p></details> | <details><summary>12 pa...</summary><p>12 pages, 1 figure, 6 tables</p></details> |
| **[Inheritance as an Admission Test for LLM Prompt Merge Operators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29023v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents are often improved by iterating until a fixed verifier is done. Once that verifier saturates, the loop has nothing left to climb. We instead score agents against the current population, and a frozen panel records whether anyone got better. The panel mean can rise inside the seeds by elimination alone. We therefore count a run as training only when its final mean beats the best generation-zero member. A merge is admitted only when the child moves toward the donor, past the child the same operator produces at zero dose, and the lower confidence bound on that movement reaches half the requested dose. That is the admission test. A detectable shortfall rejects the operator, and the test is otherwise undetermined. We keep realised fitness φas the selector and reference performance θas the unused ruler. Every measurement uses gemini-3.1-flash-lite on one negotiation task, and no fixed-oracle arm was run. In the open loop, tactic append grafts short donor rules and is admitted. Free rewrite moves only a third of the requested dose and is rejected. Template append pastes raw donor replies into the child. Its movement clears the bound on the first probe but not on the re-probe, and it is left undetermined. A held-out re-probe on the five situations the merge never saw confirms that both appends move, and it does not rescue free rewrite. Neither append rises with the dose. Constrained rewrite is admitted in the open loop and was not run in the closed loop, which changes only the operator. The population, the scores and the task stay fixed. No free-rewrite run beats the best generation-zero member. Every tactic-append run and every template-append run does. Sampled loop children keep that split. Free rewrite moves no further toward the donor, and both appends still clear the bound, by less than in the open loop.</p></details> |  |
| **[PerMIT: Securing Persistent Memory for LLM Agents via Machine-Checked Integrity Theorems and Scalable Community Trust](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28987v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Persistent memory is a largely undefended surface for injection and poisoning, and the same risk limits memory sharing among agents. We present PerMIT, a memory architecture with provable integrity guarantees under an explicit trust assumption: only user-typed inputs and values confirmed at a gate are trusted. PerMIT mediates the release and use of untrusted environment-derived values through a finite memory interface, while preserving agent-driven multi-turn planning and tool choice. Over this finite interface, we prove two integrity theorems that are also machine-checked in Lean 4, which together ensure that environment-derived memory reaches a sensitive action only if confirmed at the gate and influences the agent's behavior only through what the gate displays. Every attack in our threat model thereby reduces to a single auditable decision, which a human, an LLM verifier, or both can make in practice. On Trojan Hippo, PerMIT records zero observed attack success with self-verification and lightweight verifiers. Because each confirmation is a logged verdict on exact content, endorsements accumulate across users, and sufficiently endorsed items become community-confirmed with no further verification required. Under explicit assumptions, we prove and corroborate in simulation a co-scaling property: as the community grows, the risks of promoting poison and of wrongly evicting benign knowledge fall, while per-user verification effort does not rise. On $τ^2$-bench, communities of up to 90 autonomous agents use PerMIT to build shared knowledge bases with no direct memory injection promoted, and the resulting knowledge significantly improves new agents on held-out tasks, suggesting a path toward agents that safely self-improve at test time.</p></details> |  |
| **[The Butterfly Effect in LLM Social Simulations: From Architecture Sensitivity to Robustness Audits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28981v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A butterfly effect occurs when a local perturbation propagates through a system and produces divergence in its collective outcome. In LLM social simulations, this creates a particular challenge for scientific inference: sensitivity to implementation choices can enter agent interactions and alter the collective outcomes on which scientific claims are based. We study this problem through a repeated Prisoner's Dilemma and a social-media echo chamber, spanning representation- and design-level perturbations across four LLM families. We find that controlled perturbations can substantially alter collective outcomes, with sensitivity varying across both perturbations and models. In the Prisoner's Dilemma, changing only the representation of a matched persona shifts two-agent cooperation by 75.2 percentage points for GPT-5.2, while game framing and memory representation produce smaller or less consistent effects. Multi-agent interaction further reshapes representation sensitivity, producing amplification or attenuation depending on the model and perturbation. In the echo-chamber simulation, persona representation changes interaction targeting and is accompanied by divergence in collective interaction structure, extending representation sensitivity from agent behavior to network-level outcomes. These results show that robustness cannot be inferred from a single simulation configuration and that local sensitivity can change as it propagates through interaction. We introduce TRAILS (Taxonomy for Robustness Audits In LLM Simulations) and the Social Simulation Audit Card as a framework for systematically auditing these dependencies and calibrating the scope of scientific claims to the robustness evidence that supports them.</p></details> |  |
| **[Model Capacity Frontier: A Development Framework for LLM-Supported Social Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24168v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents are increasingly incorporated into agent-based models (ABMs) on the premise that richer, context-sensitive human behavior can improve empirical fidelity.
