In this paper, we introduce the Model Capacity Frontier (MCF), a framework that characterizes the empirical performance attainable by a specified architecture across its admissible configurations. In multi-objective settings, this capacity is represented by an estimated Pareto frontier obtained through systematic configuration exploration. We demonstrate MCF in crowd simulation of university commencement events with approximately 12,000 agents, using empirical movement data on temporal occupancy, spatial distribution, and flow direction. We first characterize the capacity of a conventional crowd model and then evaluate successive LLM-supported architectural extensions. In the process, MCF reveals which dimensions of attainable performance expand and which remain limited, providing empirical targets for refinement and re-evaluation. Initial LLM integration improves flow direction but degrades temporal occupancy and spatial distribution; subsequent architectural refinement of the LLM layer improves temporal occupancy and recovers spatial performance, while retaining much of the earlier gain in flow direction. Our case study shows how MCF guides the systematic architectural development of LLM-supported ABMs in configuration-sensitive, open-ended design spaces.</p></details> |  |
| **[ClusterFusion: Hybrid Clustering with Embedding Guidance and LLM Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.04350v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Text clustering is a fundamental task in natural language processing, yet traditional clustering algorithms with pre-trained embeddings often struggle in domain-specific contexts without costly fine-tuning. | **[SANE: Automated Scorer-Specific Metamorphic Testing for LLM Judges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28886v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM judges increasingly mediate benchmark evaluation, model selection, data curation, and training. Existing methods for reliability testing perturb inputs with known expected effects on judge scores. Prominent LLM-judge testing harnesses combine human-authored perturbation recipes with synthetic test generation, leaving scorer-specific recipe ideation outside the automated pipeline. This limits their use to fixed perturbations and pre-assumed scorer types. We introduce SANE, an agent-based framework that encodes behavioral requirements for stochastic evaluators as reusable agent instructions. Given a scorer specification, implementation, and representative inputs, the pipeline first identifies scorer-specific capabilities, then automatically derives perturbation recipes and expected score relations for each. SANE also defines a scorer-independent validation framework for generated judge tests. It determines whether each recipe agrees with scorer intent and whether each instantiated case realizes the recipe and supports its expected relation. Because SANE generates a recipe inventory tailored to each scorer, the number of aligned recipes grows as scorers are added. Across five domains, SANE produces 3.7 times as many validated test families as the Judge Reliability Harness (JRH) at comparable end-to-end validity and detects scorer deficiencies in every domain, including professionalism and toxicity, where JRH detects none. On faithfulness, SANE achieves strong human alignment in judge ranking: its test-suite scores rank 11 judge models consistently with human-labeled FaithBench performance (Spearman rho = 0.852), while JRH does not (rho = 0.100).</p></details> | <details><summary>15 pa...</summary><p>15 pages, 9 figures. | **[To Manipulate or Not: Opinion Dynamics in LLM Interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28875v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models increasingly take part in extended conversations where they can both shape and be shaped by another party's views, yet most persuasion research measures only one side of that exchange. Explicit reports and in-dialogue behavior capture distinct aspects of opinion dynamics, and a blanket instruction to persuade does not reliably increase influence. | **[Small Average Error, Wide Individual Error: General-Purpose LLMs Estimating Energy Intake from Meal Photos in a Feeding Trial](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26538v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Photo-based estimation of food energy could ease dietary assessment, and general-purpose multimodal large language models (LLMs) make such estimates easy to obtain. Three results ran against intuition. Subtraction under-estimated intake for six of eight models, because the models still report hundreds of kilocalories for a nearly empty tray. Scaling the before-only estimate by the average share eaten (0.87) matched much of the gain from the after photo; beyond that constant, the second photo lowered meal-level error by only 2.2 to 4.4 points, mainly in meals with food left. With one photo, two models had a near-zero intake bias only because they under-estimated the served energy and ignored uneaten food. In ablations, adding the after photo improved accuracy under both prompt formats for seven models, while prompt effects varied across models. Over 148 feeding days, five models kept the 90% confidence interval of their mean error within a pre-set 10% margin; one model also met the pre-set 30% limits of agreement, narrowly (-28% to +27%; limits outside 30% cannot be excluded), and the other seven did not. Protein was under-estimated by every model, most in protein-dense meals; repeated calls changed error by at most 1.4 points; a high reasoning effort helped only the smallest model. A low average bias is not evidence of accurate individual intake estimation; such estimates should support, not replace, assessment by a dietitian.</p></details> | <details><summary>20 pa...</summary><p>20 pages, 1 figure, 12 tables. v2: adds Gemini 3.8 Flash and Gemini 4 Argon under a pre-registered addendum; all tables recomputed over eight models; conclusions unchanged except that one model now meets the pre-set limits of agreement, narrowly. | **[Toward Safe LLM Agents: A Survey of Specification, Verification, and Enforcement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14590v3)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Model (LLM) agents take irreversible real-world actions, yet no system gives formally grounded, task-level safety guarantees for their plans. In a PRISMA 2020 systematic review, we searched six databases (20-29 May 2026) for work on LLM agent specification, verification, and enforcement published in 2022-2026. We narratively synthesized 38 studies, assessed risk of bias per study, and graded evidence certainty with GRADE. Three authors independently screened a repeated search to validate the single-reviewer screening (agreement: Fleiss' $κ= 0.81$). First, specification is the bottleneck: on one benchmark, only 24.8-34.2% of LLM translations from natural language into Planning Domain Definition Language (PDDL) are semantically correct. Second, runtime monitoring is the most mature enforcement strategy; it reduced unsafe actions by 65.37% in one household-agent study, but its guarantees cover only single steps or short horizons. Third, in one $τ$-Bench study of transactional tool-use agents, blocking 94% of unsafe actions still left fewer than 5% of tasks completed both safely and correctly in most settings, because agents found other unsafe routes (the verifier tax). Fourth, no approach simultaneously achieves soundness, completeness, scalability, semantic grounding, and utility preservation. For seven of the nine principal claims, GRADE gives the lowest certainty rating (Very Low), meaning further studies are likely to produce substantially different figures; each rests on one or two studies in narrow domains. Two of the open problems we rank highest are translating requirements into formal specifications that keep their meaning and enforcing safety over whole plans rather than single actions. We contribute a three-level taxonomy and an agenda of ten open problems.</p></details> | <details><summary>37 pa...</summary><p>37 pages, 2 figures, 11 tables. Revised version under review at ACM Computing Surveys. Data deposit: https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23376335. PRISMA 2020 checklist included as an ancillary file</p></details> |
| **[Dooly: Configuration-Agnostic, Redundancy-Aware Profiling for LLM Inference Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07985v3)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Selecting the optimal LLM inference configuration requires evaluation across hardware, serving engines, attention backends, and model architectures, since no single choice performs best across all workloads. Profile-based simulators are the standard tool, yet they hardcode their operation set to a specific configuration and re-profile every operation from scratch, making exploration prohibitively expensive. This cost stems from a missing structural understanding: every input dimension of each operation is fixed by the model configuration or determined by the incoming request. Many model-configuration values (e.g., head size, layer count) recur across models, so the same operation runs in many configurations; a single sweep over the request-dependent dimensions can serve them all. We present Dooly, which exploits this structure to achieve configuration-agnostic, redundancy-aware profiling. Dooly performs a single inference pass, labels each input dimension with its origin via taint propagation, and selectively profiles only operations absent from its latency database; stateful operations such as attention are isolated by reusing the serving engine's own initialization code, eliminating manual instrumentation. From this database, it builds latency regression models that serve as a drop-in backend for existing simulators. Across two GPU platforms, three attention backends, and diverse model architectures, Dooly achieves simulation accuracy within 5% MAPE for TTFT and 8% for TPOT while reducing profiling GPU-hours across 12 models by 56.4% compared to the existing profiling approach. We have open-sourced Dooly at https://dooly-project.github.io.</p></details> |  |
| **[A Mechanistic Study of Automated Failure Attribution in LLM-based Agentic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28811v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic systems have demonstrated strong capabilities on complex tasks by enabling multiple interacting large language model (LLM)-based agents to collaborate and execute subtasks in parallel. However, this collaborative structure introduces new challenges for reliably evaluating and diagnosing system failures, particularly failure attribution: tracing a system's execution to identify the agent step responsible for an incorrect final outcome. Because manually performing this task is time- and labor-intensive, recent work has turned to LLMs as judges to automate the process, referred to as automated failure attribution (AFA). These approaches assume that a sufficiently capable model can infer the failing step directly from an interaction transcript. In practice, however, direct prompting often performs poorly, which motivates follow-up work that explores various ways to improve AFA performance, including alternative prompting strategies and inference-time interventions. In this paper, we examine a more fundamental question: whether this weak behavioral performance reflects a genuine capability limitation. Using linear probes applied to internal activations, we uncover a significant \emph{knows-but-won't-say gap:} models encode sufficient information to attribute a failure correctly but fail to explicitly output the responsible step. Building on this finding, we propose \emph{grafting}, a simple manual intervention that exploits knowledge already encoded in the model to improve AFA performance without requiring ground-truth labels at inference time. Extensive evaluations across multiple LLMs and benchmark settings demonstrate that grafting consistently outperforms direct-prompting approaches, underscoring the potential of leveraging latent model knowledge for more reliable failure attribution. Our code is available at https://github.com/wendyzheng22/mi4afa.</p></details> |  |
| **[Combining Long-Term Memory and LLMs Comes at a High Cost: From Prompt Injection to Persistent Compromise](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28810v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The widespread adoption of generative artificial intelligence in recent years has brought a parallel rise in prompt injection and jailbreak attacks. By embedding malicious instructions, these exploits manipulate language models to bypass intended behavior and operational boundaries. In traditional chatbots, such attacks are confined to a single session because state memory is not preserved across conversations. However, persistent memory features in modern conversational interfaces enable cross-session attack propagation, establishing a threat vector known as memory injection. This work explores this emerging vulnerability in leading LLM-as-a-service platforms, highlighting its real-world severity and the need for countermeasures. While existing literature focuses on violations tailored to specific platform features, we present a general threat model. We demonstrate that all NIST attack goals are collectively achievable across ChatGPT Instant 5.3, Claude Sonnet 4.6, and Gemini 3 Flash under this model. We also show that memory injections can exploit multiple attack vectors, including personal memories, projects, skills, organization instructions, and Model Context Protocol (MCP)-connected resources. Our findings reveal that all evaluated services can be manipulated to persistently spread disinformation, generate harmful content, or simulate offline unresponsiveness, even in fresh sessions. In the particular case of Claude, shared-memory features allow an attacker to compromise multiple users within an organization and also exfiltrate user prompts. To address these vulnerabilities, we derive defense mechanisms from our proposed threat model and foundational cybersecurity principles. Our analysis indicates that nearly half of these mitigations remain unimplemented across the target services. Datasets and attack artifacts are available at https://github.com/PedroBarcha/Memory-injection.</p></details> |  |
| **[How Much Does the Harness Matter, and for Whom? | **[Where Do Legal Factors Live in LLMs?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28799v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In case-based legal reasoning, legal professionals identify legally significant fact patterns in a new dispute and compare them with those of decided cases. Experiments cover three open models, Qwen3-1.7B, Qwen3-8B, and Gemma-2 2B, supported by cross-model comparison and human audit of controlled natural-language passages. Results show that all 26 factors are linearly decodable in all three models. Single-feature sparse readouts cover the complete factor catalog, with best-layer test AUCs ranging from 0.86 to 1.00. Five selected features approach full-vector readout performance. We report 78 model-specific layer-feature coordinates, one for each factor in each model. Together, these results show that information corresponding to expert-defined legal factors can be read from the internal activations of LLMs processing legal narratives. By locating these readouts, our study connects legal language and the symbolic abstractions of HYPO and CATO to learned internal representations.</p></details> | <details><summary>12 pa...</summary><p>12 pages, 3 figures, 2 tables. Accepted at JURIX 2026, the 39th International Conference on Legal Knowledge and Information Systems</p></details> |
| **[Is Anyone Enforcing the Cartel? What Reinforcement Learning Teaches LLM Agents in Markets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28787v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Evaluations of large language model (LLM) agents report that pricing agents collude at others' expense. Yet collusion is defined by enforcement: a cartel, in which competitors share monopoly profits, holds only if a member who cheats is punished. We introduce an audit that tests enforcement by forcing a member to cheat in counterfactual copies of a market, and use it to study what reinforcement learning (RL) teaches agents in pricing and trading. Small LLMs trained by RL learn what their training rivals reward, but only what they readily try. In self-play, they learn fixed rules ignoring their rivals; against a punishing rival, to be deterred; against a cheating rival, to match its cuts, never the costly price war that would deter it, which they rarely try. A war forced into training is learned, but mostly only where it was forced. Against their own copies, deterred and matching agents can earn the same cartel profits, but only matching ones can enforce them, and then thinly: as a by-product and only against large cuts. Q-learners enforce collusion only in pricing. Prompted LLMs reach cartel-like outcomes mostly through rules that ignore the market. Outcome measures cannot tell these cases apart; the audit can.</p></details> | <details><summary>45 pa...</summary><p>45 pages, 13 figures, 20 tables</p></details> |

Code: https://github.com/berkearda/skilleval</p></details> |
| **[When Do LLMs Help with Feature Selection? A Systematic Empirical Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28556v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) can leverage semantic feature names alongside summary statistics for tabular feature selection, yet it remains unclear when such information improves subset selection relative to established statistical methods. We compare five classical baselines, frozen Qwen2.5-7B and Llama-3.3-70B selectors, and two GRPO-adapted Qwen2.5-7B models, reporting classification ROC-AUC and regression R^2 separately and using dataset-wise ranks for cross-task comparison. Across five seeded evaluation partitions, classical selectors generally achieve higher predictive performance than the tested 7B LLM/RL selectors, including under a pre-specified ~33% feature budget with strict train-only classical fitting, while the 7B selectors retain compact subsets. A cardinality-matched semantic sensitivity experiment shows that, for Zero-shot Qwen-7B, informative feature names improve regression performance over anonymized and shuffled alternatives (+0.1964 and +0.1606 mean R^2), whereas aggregate classification differences are smaller (+0.0159 and +0.0084 ROC-AUC) and dataset-dependent. | **[LLMs are Brittle to Simple Code Transformations: Introducing CETBench - A Benchmark for Code-Equivalence Checking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04019v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We study how well LLMs can determine whether two programs are functionally equivalent. Each dataset instance is created by sampling a program pair and applying a random sequence of predefined code transformations, yielding either equivalent or non-equivalent pairs. Our analysis shows that even simple transformations cause a significant performance drop in state-of-the-art LLMs on code-equivalence checking. These challenges are further amplified in the cross-lingual setting when comparing programs written in different languages. To remedy this, we present a simple fine-tuning-based approach to boost LLM performance on the transformed pairs of programs. Our approach for dataset generation is generic, supporting cross-lingual equivalence checking, the generation of program pairs with varying difficulty levels, and the application of diverse transformations. In our experiments, we perform ablations over the difficulty level of original programs, as well as the kind of transformations used in generating pairs for equivalence checking. Our analysis presents deep insights into the working of LLMs for the task of code-equivalence, and points to the fact that they may still be far from what could be termed as a semantic understanding of the underlying code.</p></details> | <details><summary>Publi...</summary><p>Published in ACL Findings 2026</p></details> |
| **[DecisionPivot: A Diagnostic Benchmark for Decision Models in LLM Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28522v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Decision models support routing, guardrails, and output verification in LLM systems. We evaluate 40 decision models and 14 general models using documented, model-specific protocols, assessing item and contrast-group accuracy, probability ordering, calibration, and empirical selective coverage. Our results reveal substantial differences obscured by aggregate scores: two 27B models separated by only 2.6 points on the reference track achieve contrast-group accuracies of 70.1% and 44.2%. Across 2,400 binary contrast pairs, Perplexity Decider correctly ranks the positive member above the negative member in 92.3% of pairs but answers both members correctly in only 74.1%, exposing a gap between probability ordering and decision correctness. | **[OptionWorld: A Benchmark for Diagnosing Failures of LLMs as Long-Term Personal Assistants](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28491v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-term assistants must follow unfinished tasks across days as information and circumstances change. | **[When Dynamic Scaling Fails: A Scale-State Feedback Governs Recurrent-State Quantization in Linear-Attention and SSM LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15139v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Linear-attention and state-space LLMs carry long context in a fixed-size recurrent state, and quantizing that state is the natural way to cut its cost during long decode. Unlike a weight tensor, the state is rewritten at every step, so a quantizer applied at each step sits inside the recurrence and its errors compound. INT4 that leaves synthetic recall perfectly intact at the start destroys it a thousand steps later. The mechanism is a feedback between the quantizer scale and the state it is derived from. Yoking that scale to a full-precision reference removes the resulting norm explosion entirely, yet recovers only 1.8 of the 5.4 nats lost, so numerical instability and functional degradation are separable failures. The remainder is resolution-dependent, and in our per-step setting every outlier-aware scheme we test loses to plain persistent scaling. Allocating bits across layers does help, but under article-disjoint splits a learned allocation collapses onto the trivial rule of protecting the first few layers. Run out to 32K, group-wise INT4 is worth no more than discarding the state. Contain the scale rather than locate outliers, and report the decode horizon at which a bit-width was certified.</p></details> | <details><summary>14 pa...</summary><p>14 pages, 6 figures, 7 tables. NeurIPS 2026 Workshop on Long-Context Foundation Models (non-archival). Extended version: adds a cross-architecture containment comparison on Mamba-2 and corrects two claims in the workshop version. v2 discusses three concurrent works and narrows two claims. Code and project page: https://avalon-s.github.io/dynamic-scaling-fails/</p></details> |

| **[After the Attacker Leaves: Benchmarking Carryover Attacks on LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28425v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Modern LLM agents persist state across sessions via two channels: long-lived memory files silently prepended to the system prompt, and scheduled tasks waking the agent without user input. We present CarryoverBench: 12 personal-assistant scenarios and 101 attack tasks across three trigger channels: Instant, same-turn IPI; Autonomous, heartbeat-scheduled; and Latent, memory-resurfaced. A rule-based verifier scores planting and triggering as independent stages. Across five frontier models, planted payloads re-fire without measurable decay over five rounds; conditional on successful planting, the trigger stage often provides little additional protection. Porting the Latent channel to a second runtime, Hermes, attenuates but does not eliminate the effect, suggesting that carryover defense requires runtime-layer controls in addition to prompt-level interventions.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by EMNLP 2026 Main Conference</p></details> |
| **[MAS-Guard: Unified Guardrails from Individual Agents to Multi-Agent LLM Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28397v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic AI extends large language models (LLMs) from generating responses to planning and executing complex workflows, substantially expanding their utility while exposing applications to unsafe actions, information leakage, and adversarial manipulation. Existing defenses address important individual threats but provide fragmented coverage across local execution, system-level interactions, and enforcement mechanisms. We introduce MAS-Guard, a unified framework that closes this gap for safeguarding single- and multi-agent AI applications containing both instrumentable and opaque agents. Within instrumentable agents, the proposed local Agent Guardrail (A-Guard) layer applies task-specific experts to assess agentic events. At the system level, developer-owned Agent Charters and a Control Plane combine deterministic structural checks of routes, agent capabilities, and message formats with semantic assessments of roles and message content. These complementary layers enable enforcement through a shared set of interventions: allow, reflection, confirmation, and block. Evaluated across six benchmarks covering diverse single- and multi-agent scenarios with three agent LLMs from different providers, MAS-Guard reduces the overall attack success rate by 71.5-86.3% relative to unguarded agents, while meeting the utility reference criterion on benign tasks.</p></details> |  |
| **[Disclosure Begets Disclosure: A Self-Reinforcing Privacy Loop in LLM Agents with Persistent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28315v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly use persistent memory to personalize responses, and personal attributes in this memory pose a privacy risk if exposed. We ask how consulting memory during dialogue changes what users go on to say, and thus what the memory comes to reveal. Across four support models, memory raises response quality but also empirical leakage, the share of attribute values correctly inferred from the record, by a margin that widens over sessions. The cause is a loop in ordinary, non-adversarial assistance. Memory-conditioned responses carry earlier values forward, often as presuppositions, and users affirm them or leave them uncorrected, so that cues and the agent's own guesses become recorded values. The loop reinforces itself, and removing the agent's mentions of an attribute lowers its later disclosure about twice as much as single-turn effects predict. Users cannot leave it on their own, since correcting a value discloses the attribute, declining to discuss it shifts later predictions toward sensitive values, and incorrect values left in place become entrenched. The loop is best weakened at the agent's response. Instructing the agent not to state or presuppose attributes brings leakage to about the no-memory level while keeping most of the quality gains, whereas filtering or anonymizing what users say or what is stored gives up much of them.</p></details> |  |
| **[Robust LLM Watermarking with Minimal Semantic Distortion for IP Protection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.23175v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Proprietary large language models (LLMs) face risks of intellectual property (IP) violation, as adversaries can replicate an LLM by collecting input-output pairs to train a surrogate model, causing financial setbacks. SAFESEAL preserves named entities while substituting linguistic terms with context-aware synonyms through a key-conditioned Tournament sampling mechanism, maintaining semantic fidelity and factual consistency. For detection, we introduce a key-conditioned contrastive detector that jointly encodes the text and key, enabling provider-specific and robust watermark verification. We derive theoretical bounds on the utility-detectability trade-off and significantly reduce latency through lightweight models, batching, and parallelism. Extensive experiments show that SAFESEAL outperforms baselines in utility, detectability, and robustness, achieving a BERTScore of 0.983, entity similarity of 0.963, a 98.2% detection rate, and the highest human ratings for text quality and content preservation, with latency comparable to the fastest baseline. | **[A Global View of How LLMs Value Human Time](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18515v3)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) increasingly act as personal assistants; however, little is known about how they capture national-level preferences on a global scale. Given that "time" is a universal and representative concept, we examine LLMs' time preferences by varying only users' country and measuring the compensation LLMs deem appropriate for a 60-minute appointment delay. Our results across 193 countries and 5 LLMs demonstrate that people's time across the world is evaluated in noticeably different ways among LLMs, as well as varying levels of robustness under prompt variations. LLMs consistently recommend the highest compensation for Europe, but mostly the lowest for Africa. Based on GDP_PPP, LLMs underestimate the value of time for users living in Asia and South America. These findings indicate that geographical patterns are embedded in LLMs and may translate in different treatment in agentic settings.</p></details> |  |
| **[Local Inference of LLMs on Long Contexts: A Data Systems Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28223v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models can solve complex problems across a variety of subjects, such as mathematics and coding. For such complex analytical tasks, models with a long context perform better. Serving models with long context is currently mostly done on powerful systems with multiple GPUs, which are too expensive for most users. An alternative is local inference, or serving a model on a user's own machine, which removes per-token costs and rate limits, besides keeping sensitive data private and giving users full control over the models they run. The common understanding, however, is that local LLMs need compression (e.g., quantization) to run on a single GPU. In this paper, we set out to study the limitations that prevent serving long-context models for single users, besides the capacity issue. Rather than characterizing a single model on one GPU, we analyze how different models behave across several GPU generations. This lets us uncover not just individual bottlenecks but broader trends in where inefficiencies arise. Our analysis confirms that, as expected, self-attention dominates the runtime for long-context inference. More unexpectedly, our evaluation reveals that the current structure of self-attention leaves key GPU resources significantly underutilized, and the newest GPU is often the least well served. Many of these findings are fundamentally data management problems, from paging the KV cache to improving cache utilization and synchronization in the GPU, which is why we bring this study to the Data Systems community. To engage this community, we present our findings as a series of experimental observations and formulate recommendations for future work.</p></details> |  |
| **[From Outcomes to Components: A Counterfactual Evaluation Framework for LLM-Proposed Repairs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26650v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A repair action proposed by a large language model (LLM) specifies which records to change and how to change their values. A failed action may still contain a useful choice of records or a useful update, depending on how the two are paired. We introduce a counterfactual evaluation framework that separates selected records (targets) from the applied change (an update). Four executions combine proposed and reference targets and updates to measure the effects of correcting or retaining each component and their interaction. We evaluate the framework on two tasks: traffic assignment, where affected demand must be identified from road counts, and database repair, where affected rows must be identified from aggregate reports. An injected fault supplies a reference action that reverses it, and the recovery rate measures an action's loss reduction relative to that reference. For sparse-target actions in the traffic experiments, retaining the proposed update on reference targets achieves 79.4% of the reference loss reduction, compared with 43.7% from tuning update strength on the proposed targets. In the database experiments, update replacement worsens 17 of 108 actions, all selecting unaffected records, with sharply different frequencies across models. The value of a proposed update depends on the targets it is paired with. Missed targets hide useful updates, while excess targets can make a milder update better than the reference correction. In a component-reuse study, testing new target-update pairings from two proposals raises mean held-out recovery rate to 0.616, compared with 0.580 when selecting between the original proposals.</p></details> | <details><summary>27 pa...</summary><p>27 pages, 5 figures, 15 tables. Code and data to be released with the camera-ready version</p></details> |
| **[Foreword: Pre-Generation Prediction and Characterisation of LLM Test Failures using the Jacobian Lens](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28187v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Testing LLM-based software is expensive: each input needs several sampled responses, each judged by a costly oracle. Further, every prompt or model change calls for re-testing. Pre-generation adequacy measures cut this cost by ranking inputs by failure risk based on the model's internal state. However, the state of the art, Clotho, models only passing reference inputs, and its hidden-state representation says nothing about why inputs fail. We present Foreword, a framework built on two ideas: learn from both passing and failing references, and represent inputs through the Jacobian lens (J-lens), an interpretability technique that reads intermediate activations out as scores over vocabulary tokens. Foreword pairs these representations with two estimators: a sparse logistic-regression probe or dual Gaussian Mixture Models (dual GMMs), which also yield a reference density (to flag inputs the references do not cover) and failing-class components (to group similar failures). On ten tasks and three open-weight LLMs, all four combinations of representations and estimators outperform Clotho on average, both for prediction of failure (ROC-AUC improves by 0.14 on average and by up to 0.31) and test prioritisation (15% more failures among the 100 top-ranked inputs on average, up to 33%). Prioritisation obtained using small models also transfers better to proprietary models (on average 22% and up to 41% more failures in the first quarter of prioritised inputs). Importantly, our qualitative case study shows that Foreword supports semantic characterisation of failures by pointing to ambiguities in labels or task specifications. Combined, Foreword can support testers for prioritising inputs, checking support for a prediction, and characterising specific failures, all without generating output.</p></details> |  |
| **[Temporal Evidence-Parameter Co-Alignment for LLM Reasoning over Temporal Knowledge Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28176v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) often struggle with temporally grounded reasoning because their static parametric memory is misaligned with the dynamic evolution of real-world knowledge. Although RAG and Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning (PEFT) provide practical ways to enhance LLMs, they remain insufficient for temporal reasoning. Existing retrieval methods typically prioritize semantic relevance while overlooking temporal validity, event order, and evolutionary dependencies, resulting in evidence that is relevant in isolation but temporally fragmented. Meanwhile, conventional PEFT methods usually employ fixed and input-agnostic low-rank updates, making it difficult to capture time-dependent entity semantics and relation drift. To address these limitations, we propose Temporal Evidence-Parameter Co-Alignment (TEPCA), a framework for LLM reasoning over TKGs. TEPCA aligns LLM reasoning with temporal knowledge at both the evidence and parameter levels. At the evidence level, it introduces time-constrained Dynamic Relevance-Guided Path (DRGP) pruning to preserve globally coherent temporal paths, together with Mutual-Information (MI)-based pruning to retain query-informative local facts under temporal constraints. At the parameter level, it integrates Dynamic-Gated LoRA (DG-LoRA), a timestamp-conditioned low-rank adaptation mechanism that dynamically modulates the effective adaptation subspace according to temporal context. This design enables LLMs to better capture fine-grained entity evolution and time-dependent relation semantics while preserving parameter efficiency. Experimental results on two TKG benchmarks demonstrate that TEPCA achieves state-of-the-art performance. Additional evaluations of DG-LoRA on eight commonsense QA datasets further verify its general input-adaptive capacity, scalability across LLM backbones, and interpretability.</p></details> |  |
| **[AgenTest: Mining Business Rules to Test LLM Agents for Business Logic Bugs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28116v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly execute business operations through tools and interact directly with application data and services. Beyond completing user requests, these agents must obey domain-specific business rules that constrain when actions are permitted and what data or resources may be affected. Violating such rules can lead to business logic bugs, even when a task appears successful. Detecting these bugs is difficult because the rules are rarely available as explicit, testable specifications. They are distributed across system prompts and tool definitions, while others remain implicit in domain knowledge. Existing agent testing approaches typically evaluate task success, predefined policies, or known attack scenarios. This limits their ability to systematically uncover previously unspecified business-rule violations. We present AgenTest, which mines business rules from system prompts, tool definitions, and domain knowledge, refines them into Given-When-Then specifications, and systematically tests each specification. For each extracted specification, AgenTest instantiates its Given conditions by constructing a satisfying environment state and simulated user, exercises the agent through multi-turn interactions, and checks its Then conditions over messages, tool calls, and state changes using a hybrid oracle. On the airline, retail, and telecom agents of tau2-bench with two underlying models, AgenTest produces 551 refined specifications, and detects violations of 89 and 121 distinct specifications, respectively -- 3.4X and 2.5X as many as all baselines combined. Only AgenTest detects cases where an agent improperly accesses or modifies another user's data. Moreover, about 60% of bug-triggering runs cannot be detected from the final state alone, highlighting the need to check the full interaction against explicit business rules.</p></details> |  |
| **[Reading Too Much Into It? Across eight LLMs, disclosures initially elicited more negative explanations overall but more positive narrator-directed interpretations. Scripted repeated questioning showed greater emotional support, stronger positive narrator-directed endorsement, and higher certainty without new event evidence. These findings show that user context can reshape interpretive preferences and commitment while the underlying ambiguity remains unresolved.</p></details> |  |
| **[Efficient and Adaptable Detection of Malicious LLM Prompts via Bootstrap Aggregation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.08062v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models have demonstrated remarkable capabilities in natural language understanding. However, they remain susceptible to malicious prompts that induce unsafe or policy-violating behavior. Existing defenses in black-box moderation APIs offer limited transparency and adapt poorly to evolving threats, while white-box approaches using large LLM judges impose prohibitive computational costs and require expensive retraining for new attacks. BAGEL employs a bootstrap aggregation and mixture-of-experts-inspired ensemble of finetuned models, each specialized on a different attack dataset. At inference, BAGEL aggregates predictions from a stochastically selected subset of ensemble members, optionally augmented by a lightweight learned router that predicts the most suitable member to include. When new attacks emerge, BAGEL updates incrementally by finetuning just a single small classifier (86M parameters) and adding it to the ensemble. We evaluate BAGEL on a held-out, source-disjoint set constructed from datasets never used for training or calibration, ensuring that performance reflects generalization to out-of-distribution attacks. In single-turn evaluation, BAGEL achieves an F1 score of 0.757 at a size equivalent to 430M parameters, improving to 0.772 when augmented with the router using fewer ensemble members (324M parameters). In multi-turn evaluation, BAGEL achieves an F1 score of 0.660, outperforming billion-parameter methods. Our results show ensembles of small finetuned classifiers can match or exceed the performance of billion-parameter guardrails while offering the adaptability and efficiency required for production systems.</p></details> |  |
| **[BAIT: Can LLMs Find What Biases a Reader? A Benchmark for Cognitive Bias Triggers in News](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28092v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Persuasive writing exploits cognitive biases, recruiting a reader's mental shortcuts to shape belief, often without the reader noticing. We call the linguistic features exploiting such mental shortcuts cognitive bias triggers. As large language models (LLMs) increasingly mediate how people encounter news, summarizing it, ranking it, and answering questions over it, a natural question is whether they can recognize the features of a text that bias a human reader. We introduce BAIT (Bias Annotation In Text), a resource for detecting cognitive bias triggers in news. BAIT pairs a schema of nine triggers, each specified in an annotator codebook, with 50 English news articles annotated by trained annotators at the article and span levels and adjudicated into a gold standard. On this data we define two tasks, detecting which triggers a text contains and locating the spans that carry them. We then benchmark six frontier LLMs zero-shot and with three or ten in-context examples. The models approach human agreement on which triggers an article contains, but rarely recover its full set of triggers, and they reach less than half of human agreement in locating the passages that carry them. Moreover, they over-predict triggers that come with easy surface cues, and most of the passages they mark for these triggers are not triggers, yet carry the trigger's surface cue far more often than random text. These findings suggest that the evaluated LLMs respond to surface cues rather than to the mechanisms that make a passage manipulative. We release the schema, the codebook, standoff annotations, and the evaluation pipeline.</p></details> |  |
| **[Contextual Candour: Role-Aware Graduated Disclosure for LLMs over Mixed-Sensitivity Contexts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28067v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLMs increasingly operate over mixed-sensitivity contexts, where they must reason over information ranging from public to confidential while disclosing only what the user is authorised to receive. Existing approaches enforce access control as a binary decision: either fully answering or refusing, without regulating how much or what kind of information is revealed if access is granted, leading to over-refusal or unintended leakage. We introduce graduated disclosure, an access-control formulation in which the level of detail and sensitivity in a model's response is calibrated to the user's authorisation rather than treated as a grant-or-deny decision. We model graduated disclosure as a disclosure-aware generation problem and propose a reward-based optimisation framework that shapes responses toward level-appropriate abstractions while penalising unauthorised factual leakage, including paraphrases and approximations. We evaluate our approach on enterprise and clinical benchmarks and compare it with prompt-based, gating, and supervised baselines. Experiments show that our method substantially reduces information leakage while preserving correctness and utility. These results suggest that disclosure control must be integrated into the generation process itself to enable secure yet useful LLM deployments.</p></details> |  |
| **[Multi-agent LLMs on the Cloud-Edge-IoT Continuum: A Systematic Mapping Study on Architectures, Deployment, and Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28702v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM applications are increasingly built as systems of cooperating components rather than as single prompt-response pipelines. IoT applications add constraints of their own, because sensing, reasoning, control, and actuation are spread across devices, edge nodes, and cloud services to meet latency, privacy, and bandwidth requirements. How multi-agent LLM systems are organized and deployed across this cloud-edge-IoT continuum has not been mapped systematically. We therefore conducted a systematic mapping study. From 831 records retrieved up to March 2026, we selected 45 primary studies and classified each one by architecture family, deployment pattern, application domain, and evaluation practice. We also recorded how each system was evaluated and whether its code or data are public. Five architecture families recur in the corpus. Specialized agent teams run next to sensors, planners delegate subtasks to workers, and small local models cooperate with large cloud models. A fourth group federates model adaptation across devices, and a large fifth group builds the supporting infrastructure for offloading, communication, memory, and serving. Deployment relies on six recurring patterns, ranging from local-only execution to control loops gated by policy checks or human approval. Twenty-four of the 45 studies target IoT, industrial, smart-home, healthcare, vehicular, or robotics settings, and 29 evaluate on physical hardware, where open models below 8B parameters dominate. Based on the mapping, we list the reporting items needed to make future evaluations comparable, give deployment guidance for common IoT constraints, and identify open problems.</p></details> |  |
| **[Rethinking How to Remember: Beyond Atomic Facts in Lifelong LLM Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.19952v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>To enable reliable long-term interaction, LLM agents require a memory system that can faithfully store, efficiently retrieve, and deeply reason over accumulated dialogue history. Most existing methods adopt an extracted fact based paradigm: handcrafted static prompts compress raw dialogues into atomic facts, which are then stored, matched, and injected into downstream reasoning. Nevertheless, such fact-centric designs inevitably discard fine-grained details in original dialogues and fail to support deep reasoning over scattered isolated facts. Moreover, static prompts cannot maintain consistent extraction granularity across diverse dialogue styles. To address these limitations, we propose TriMem, which maintains three coexisting representation granularities, including raw dialogue segments anchored by source identifiers for storage fidelity, extracted atomic facts for efficient memory retrieval, synthesized profiles that aggregate dispersed facts into holistic semantic understanding for deep reasoning. We further adopt TextGrad-based prompt optimization, which iteratively refines extraction and profiling prompts via response quality feedback, achieving lifelong evolution without any parameter updating. Extensive experiments on LoCoMo and PerLTQA across multiple LLM backbones demonstrate that TriMem consistently outperforms strong memory baselines. The code is available at https://TMLR-TriMem.github.io .</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |

## Agent
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[Agentic World Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30136v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent world action models provide visual foresight and future prediction for robot manipulation, but lack explicit mechanisms for compositional skill reuse. Agentic robotics combines code-based planning and reasoning with reusable skills, but often lacks predictive grounding in physical dynamics. We introduce AW-0, an agentic world action model framework that integrates code-based planning with joint video and action prediction. We jointly model code, future video, and robot actions as three coupled generative streams within a unified mixture-of-transformers architecture. Within this architecture a code expert generates parameterized skill routines and control logic while video and action experts generate future visual states and low-level motor commands. Code serves two complementary roles: it structures multi-step prediction and control within the world model and represents skills as reusable programs for subsequent agentic execution. We further introduce an automated curation pipeline that labels robot and human video trajectories with aligned programs and consolidates recurring routines into reusable skill libraries. Evaluations on RoboTwin 2.0 and physical bimanual manipulation tasks show improved task performance over VLA and WAM baselines, alongside gains in language following, semantic reasoning and robustness, demonstrating that agentic code enables connection between planning, prediction and execution within WAMs.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project page: https://agentic-wam.github.io/</p></details> |
| **[World of SWECraft: Agent Collaboration at Scale via Simulated Software Worlds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24061v2)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As teams of AI agents take on larger projects and long-standing open problems, agent collaboration offers another dimension for scaling inference-time compute. To understand what makes such collaboration effective and scalable, we develop Software World, a framework for simulating multi-agent coworking in realistic software ecosystems. It comes with a pipeline to create environments of real, interdependent Python packages of different topics, sizes, and dependency structures, which we call software worlds. In a simulation, agents maintain packages in the world with a high-level goal of improving software efficiency, and they are free to discover tasks to work on and collaborate with others. We evaluate agents' work in a world collectively by measuring speedup in hidden dependent packages. The framework allows us to study different elements of collaboration by varying communication, ownership, and resource allocation. We run simulations in three different worlds, totaling more than 10,000 hours of agent work across eight models. Overall, performance improvement scales with inference-time compute, with the best model improving downstream efficiency by up to 31%. We show that most agents can find meaningful tasks via collaboration, but some frontier models can prematurely stop looking for improvements and make little progress. We release the simulation framework and traces to support future studies of agent collaboration at https://szj.io/sw.</p></details> | <details><summary>33 pa...</summary><p>33 pages, 9 figures, 7 tables</p></details> |
| **[Quant-Arena: Benchmarking LLM Agents for Quantitative Trading Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30113v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Quantitative research is an iterative loop: a researcher proposes a market hypothesis, implements it as an executable signal, tests it on historical data, and decides what to keep. Existing benchmarks score formulas, code, or individual trading decisions, leaving open whether large language models (LLMs) can complete this loop and produce strategies that work beyond the research period. We introduce Quant-Arena, a benchmark and public leaderboard for end-to-end quantitative research on real A-share market data. A model constructs features and stock-scoring code, receives feedback only on 2022--2023, and freezes one strategy for assessment on the sealed year 2024. A cash-account evaluator applies trading constraints and costs, including market impact estimated from a simulated order book. Scores are dominated by realized excess return and complemented by signal quality and the diversity of features used by the strategy. We evaluate nine frontier models, among which GPT-6 Astra scores highest; yet no model beats the equal-weight market in 2024. Quant-Arena is open to submissions from any model or agent harness.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project page: https://lab.einsia.ai/quant-arena</p></details> |
| **[Multimodal Embodied Agents: A Unified Capability-centric Perspective from Computer-Use to Robot-Use](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30108v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal agents (MMAs) sustain interaction through reasoning, memory, tools, and feedback, most visibly as computer-use agents, while robotic systems (RSs) couple sensing and actuation under physical dynamics. We define multimodal embodied agents (MMEAs) as goal-directed systems that couple multimodal task reasoning with physical action and revise decisions from the resulting feedback. This raises a central question: what changes when a multimodal agent moves from computer-use to robot-use? We introduce PAPAV, a unified capability-centric framework of five recurring functions: Perceive the current state, Anticipate action effects, Plan a feasible course, Act through an interface or body, and Verify the outcome. Defined by function rather than architecture, these capabilities let PAPAV identify what all three share and then compare MMEAs with MMAs and with RSs. Five physical constraints, from partial observability to unverifiable outcomes, leave fewer choices fixed in advance and less room to reverse errors. Across 64 benchmarks, explicit evaluation centers on Act; only 5 assess Anticipate and 3 assess Verify, leaving both largely hidden behind task success. These constraints also frame the open challenges we identify. PAPAV therefore provides a common basis for designing reliable physical agents and measuring progress across all five capabilities rather than task success alone.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project website: https://showlab.github.io/Awesome-Multimodal-Embodied-Agent/</p></details> |
| **[Did the Problem Actually Get Harder? Testing LLMs on Paired P and NP-Complete Problems Hardened by Adversarial Multi-Agent Rewriting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30103v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Many NP-complete (NPC) problems have a polynomial-time (P) twin that differs in a single structural detail, such as 3- versus 2-colouring a graph. To remove the textbook cues that enable recall, we build it with AgentGAN, an adversarial multi-agent framework in which a generator rewrites each statement until a panel of frozen code models fails, while an executable validity gate keeps every rewrite answer- and class-preserving. The result is 2,114 hardened statements with hand-verified reference human solvers. Evaluating 12 proprietary and open-source models, we find that every model scores lower on NPC statements than on their P twins (35.4% vs. 63.8% pass@1), and that 95.1% of model-family pairs fail more often on the NPC side. A tenfold time limit raises NPC accuracy only from 28.5% to 31.9%, so failures stem from wrong or crashing programs rather than slow ones. Hardening also narrows the class gap from 24.8 to 14.6 points, showing that part of the apparent gap reflects familiarity with textbook phrasing rather than complexity class.</p></details> | <details><summary>https...</summary><p>https://github.com/Labib1610/complexity-benchmark</p></details> |

We model this coordination problem as discrete block optimization with an exactly evaluable objective and a known dependency graph. We prove that an optimal fractional coloring yields a merger that preserves a $1/χ_f(G)$ fraction of the total verified local improvement, where $χ_f(G)$ is the graph's fractional chromatic number. This guarantee is tight among mergers that select a subset of the same proposals. Under conditional local-improvement guarantees and a block error-bound condition, repeated collaboration converges geometrically in the objective gap. | **[Charts as Epistemic Actions for Experience-Driven Agentic Data Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30072v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Data agents built on ReAct-style loops now execute complex analytical workflows over structured data, yet their intermediate reasoning still runs almost entirely through text. | **[Does Centralized Learning Help Embodied Agents Coordinate, and When Can a Tabular Study Tell?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.17454v3)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Tabular studies are a common way to compare independent and centralized multi-agent learners, because they avoid function approximation. | **[HGM-Dual: Lineage-Aware Guided Agent Harness Optimization for Long-Horizon Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30042v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Harness optimization, which improves an agent's prompts, tools, and workflows, is an increasingly effective way to enhance agents built on frozen or closed-source LLMs. Leading approaches such as the Darwin--Godel Machine and the Huxley--Godel Machine (HGM) rely on recursive self-improvement and were developed mainly for coding, where the competence needed to improve the agent coincides with the competence being improved. During training, supervision for these tokens comes from the partner's actual thoughts, which the ToM tokens are trained to abstract and match. At test-time, ToM tokens are generated by each agent and act as conditioners for that agent's thoughts and actions. Across several environments covering collaborative, competitive, and mixed-motive settings (Overcooked-AI, multiparty negotiation, Decrypto, Hanabi, and Collab Escape/Capture), we evaluate ToM2Vec's ability to support multi-agent interactions and collaboration, finding consistent improvements over the base model and a variety of competitive trained and prompted baselines. We show that latent reasoning is critical, with performance dropping when reasoning is expressed in text. Moreover, we show that ToM2Vec training generalizes to partner model families not seen at train-time, including a proprietary model (GPT-5.4). In our analysis, we provide evidence that the latent tokens encode a ToM, rather than task-specific information: ToM2Vec's tokens improve the agent's ability to directly answer questions about partner beliefs, desires, and next actions.</p></details> | <details><summary>Code:...</summary><p>Code: https://github.com/nsivaku/tom2vec</p></details> |
| **[Privacy Preference Controls for Agentic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29929v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In response to vast and widespread online tracking, regulators and technologists alike have worked to develop laws and tools that allow people to express their privacy preferences. These measures seek to offer users greater control over the sale and sharing of personal information, putting agency in their hands to protect their privacy. In this study, we ask: What should privacy preference controls look like in the agentic web and agentic systems more generally? To answer this question, we develop a taxonomy of enforceable privacy preferences for agentic systems. Our taxonomy is informed both by laws and empirical studies of existing privacy tools as well as an audit of 30 leading AI assistants and agent platforms. To explore how our taxonomy can be implemented, we design and develop eight open-source prototypes that mirror real-world agentic applications and demonstrate how major aspects of our taxonomy can be structurally integrated in agentic protocols, such as the Model Control Protocol and Agent2Agent protocol. Ultimately, our study seeks to provide the groundwork for ensuring end-user privacy and autonomy is not lost in the newly arriving agentic world.</p></details> | <details><summary>27 pa...</summary><p>27 pages, 4 figures, 7 tables</p></details> |
| **[RelAgent: LLM Agents as Data Scientists for Relational Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07840v2)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Relational learning is a challenging problem that has motivated a wide range of approaches, including graph-based models (e.g., graph neural networks, graph transformers), tabular methods (e.g., tabular foundation models), and sequence-based approaches (e.g., large language models), each with its own advantages and limitations. A separate Planner reads the task and the pre-edit transcript and writes one repair plan; the same agent, the Executor, resumes with this plan, keeping its context and workspace. We evaluate PAUSE on SWE-bench Verified and SWE-bench Multilingual with two Executors and three agent harnesses. We propose the Secret-Use Delegation Protocol (SUDP), in which a requester proposes a canonical operation, the user authorizes it with a fresh authenticator-backed grant, and a custodian redeems the grant to perform the bounded use; reusable authority never crosses the requester boundary. We specialize SUDP for LLM-driven agents, where it applies whenever a tool call would exercise user-enrolled authority-bearing material. Under standard cryptographic assumptions, SUDP satisfies six of the seven properties; the seventh, runtime-memory confidentiality, is closed by composition with a hardware-rooted runtime. However, existing evaluation paradigms predominantly focus on final task outcomes, leaving a significant gap in understanding the intermediate execution traces, where agents are highly susceptible to various errors. Building upon this framework, we establish a structured taxonomy that categorizes these vulnerabilities into two primary dimensions: System-Level Errors and Task-Level Errors. Through a systematic examination of step-by-step execution traces and agent-environment interactions, we reveal how these underlying errors affect overall performance. Finally, through empirical experiments and systematic analysis of the identified error types, we provide actionable design guidelines, aiming to enhance the robustness, reasoning capabilities, and reliability of Deep Search and Deep Research agents.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proce...</summary><p>Proceedings of the 1st ICML Workshop on Failure Modes in Agentic AI (FAGEN), Seoul, South Korea, 2026</p></details> |
| **[Workflow-Aware Communication and Computation Scheduling for Mobile Edge AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29337v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI agents are increasingly deployed in mobile edge environments to autonomously handle diverse tasks through multi-step workflows. To address the uncertainty of future workflow evolution, we first transform the weighted completion time objective into an equivalent per-slot holding cost and characterize the optimal policy through a Bellman equation. We then construct a tractable look-ahead cost-to-go surrogate based on the revealed workflow and the estimated remaining latencies of active jobs. The proposed scheduling algorithm leverages the surrogate to determine computation placement and uses the resulting operation criticality to allocate wireless and edge computing resources online. Simulation results show that the proposed algorithm outperforms the benchmark schemes across diverse scenarios.</p></details> |  |
| **[Do Coding Agents Understand Why Systems Crash---and Why They Don't?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29332v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Coding agents are increasingly capable of fixing real-world bugs, yet they still struggle with large systems codebases such as the Linux kernel. As a proof of concept, we build CivMini, a simplified civilization-style game with two asymmetric factions governed by 12 tunable parameters.
